Introduction
In 1944, Reich's friend A.S. Neill wrote:

The old saying that science can analyze the colors in a
                    Van Gogh painting is not as trite as it might
                    appear at first glance. For science, a sunset
                    is the result of dust in the air, and no amount
                    of learned science will ever help us understand
                    the emotions that a sunset can evoke in us. (…)
                    There is not one among my numerous scientific
                    friends to whom I would turn if it were a human
                    problem, not one whose judgment of a play or a
                    painting I would likely accept. It comes down to
                    the elementary fact that there can be no two
                    opinions about the effect of an acid on a metal,
                    whereas a thousand opinions about a painting or
                    a religion or a political creed. All knowledge
                    of the acids and alkalis of the world must remain
                    comparatively unimportant as long as we do not
                    possess an equally great understanding of mental
                    and emotional things. (Neill 1944, p. 68)

This passage is more substantial than it might sound at first glance. It is simply
about the innermost nature of the armored society. It is based on the fact that, for
example, love and sexuality are separated from each other. Accordingly, the "fine
arts," religion, and the humanities are separated from the natural sciences. Goethe
was perhaps the last to strive for a unity of the two spheres. He had to fail because
he knew nothing of the cause of this fateful split.


---


The chemical analysis of Van Gogh's colors is quite irrelevant to the student of
orgonomy. The main instrument of the orgonomic physicist is his subjectivity, his
body feeling, his perceptive faculty, his "orgonotic sense," and in this sense the
paintings of Van Gogh and his inner struggle can be an inspiration. Van Gogh
painted the atmospheric orgone energy, which is inseparably connected to our
emotions through its various states of excitation. The student of orgonomy knows
that every sunset is an energetically highly dramatic event and that the colors of
the sky cannot be explained merely mechanically. He sees the excitation of the
atmospheric orgone energy directly at sunset, for example when he walks through
a park at that time, and experimentally the corresponding phenomenon in the
VACOR tube. As Reich said: he could not have achieved a single formulation of
orgone physics had he not first dealt with individual and social armoring
("Character Analysis and Sexpol").

Nothing stands outside of science, neither art nor religion. For example, one can
understand modern "art" and modern "religion" (the spreading Buddhism) only if
one knows that they are a secondary expression of a particular bioenergetic
transformation, which I have explained in detail in The Mass Psychology of
Buddhism (www.orgonomie.net/hdobuddha.htm): Emotion → Sensation. This means
that religion and potentially everything else "spiritual" and emotional can be
explained orgone-bio-physically, or simply physically.

And as for the domain of mechanistic science, i.e., the handling of machines and
the transfer of the man-made machine model onto nature, it undoubtedly has its
justification. It created, for example, the notebook on which I am writing this
right now, but here I must agree with Neill's remarks in the quoted book Hearts
not Heads in the School, which revolve around the idea that in education the heart
should play first fiddle and the head is secondary. The enormous means of power
that mechanistic science has given us can only be used responsibly by people with
"education of the heart" or rather "open hearts," i.e., unarmored people, or people
who are familiar with the Emotional Pest, armoring, and orgone. Anything else
will sooner or later destroy humanity. It is like heartless sexuality, which has left
us with sexually transmitted diseases, destroyed families, and destroyed people.

1. Biophilosophy
1.a. Plato and Aristotle
"Reichians" often state that concepts must be pictorially intuitive and causal in
order to be grasped emotionally. This is said to be Reich's "functional" approach.
It ends in the notion that the universe is, so to speak, a large soup pot ("cosmic
orgone energy ocean") in which everything and anything can be explained through
vortices, thickening of the soup into lumps ("matter"), or their dissolution.
Superficially viewed, Reich describes such a world. But if one looks more closely,
this is only his attempt to express himself as generally comprehensibly as
possible.1 Similarly, General Relativity Theory, the most complex theory ever
devised, is illustrated with the famously simple rubber sheet grid over which metal
balls roll. But one can understand General Relativity only after many years of
familiarizing oneself with the language of formulas and being able to apply it to
solve concrete physical problems. As a non-physicist, Reich's approach was
fundamentally different, but here too the generally accessible presentation should
not be confused with the actual theory.

Physics is not pictorial and, properly understood, not causal. For example, one
calculates how many people fit into a hall. For this one takes not further-defined
"objects" that have neither color, mass, nor anything else, but only the average
width and depth of a person, a minimum distance between these "objects"
specified from another side, and calculates. To communicate this calculation to
schoolchildren, these objects must have a certain form and color, perhaps
Playmobil figures, but this profoundly contradicts the "abstracting" nature of
physics. For example, one would place the Playmobil figures one after another
into the hall in an educational film, whereas for the calculation, i.e., the actual
physics, they are, so to speak, non-causally simply there, especially since they
have neither form nor color nor anything else besides the two dimensions of
width and depth. What does not appear in the formulas with which the given
problem is formulated and ultimately calculated is of no interest!2


---

It is the same with orgone physics. How one imagines, for example, the pictorially-
causal mechanism that causes the temperature in the orgone energy accumulator to
rise is ultimately irrelevant. What is of practical importance is that the temperature
difference To-T decreases during atmospheric contraction, at night, and in "bad
weather," and increases during the day and in "good weather," i.e., it is linked to
the functions "contraction" and "expansion." The decisive difference from
conventional physics is not only the concept of orgonotic pulsation, but above all
that the subjective assessments "bad weather" and "good weather" correlate better
with the course of To-T than, say, measurements with a barometer and
hygrometer. To introduce pictorially intuitive and mechanically causal elements
here, as seems to be the hobby of "Reichians," only distracts from this crucial
point: that the (unarmored) organism is the best measuring instrument — because it
does not work (primarily) like a machine in a pictorially-causal comprehensible
(mechanical) way, but as a functional configuration. Accordingly, a physicist will
never understand why one should treat an orgone energy accumulator as carefully
as a living organism if one wants to obtain reasonable measurement results. Or
when one claims that Hamburg is poorly suited for orgone research because the
atmosphere is "too humid," and that, say, Bremen is much better suited, which one
can say almost as soon as one has been in that city for half an hour. A physicist is
conditioned to believe that the laws of nature work the same way mechanically at
all locations and that subjective assessments are irrelevant or misleading. After
all, this is how physics emancipated itself from Aristotelian natural philosophy.


---


The separation of quantity from quality is the foundation of physics. Through it,
physics freed itself from Aristotle and became a (mechanistic) science. For
example, the color blue and its relation to the color red are of no interest
whatsoever, only that "blue" photons are more energetic than "red" ones. That red
is a signal color and blue has something of a melancholic effect is of no concern
to physicists and must be left out. For Reich, however, the dissolution of the
opposition between quantity and quality was precisely the starting point of
orgone biophysics.

Physicists readily concede that Aristotle may have been a good psychologist and
biologist, if only he had not transferred his psychological and biological thinking
to purely physical phenomena. But for both Aristotle and Reich, it was not the
task of psychology and biology to follow physics, but the reverse. Reich wrote in
1944 in a letter to his colleague Theodore Wolfe:

It is no accident that orgone energy — i.e., the
                     fundamental cosmic energy that governs life — was
                     discovered not in the context of the physics of dead matter,
                     but in the context of psychiatry, of emotions. In making the
                     discovery, I in no way left the domain of bio-psychiatry. The
                     relationship between physical-mathematical science and the
                     science of life is simply reversed: until now, biology,
                     medicine, etc., have followed chemistry and physics and tried
                     in a completely misguided way to ground their science on

In my view, this is the specific reason why almost every physicist reacts almost
automatically so negatively to Reich. After all, as has been said, physics
developed through emancipation from the Aristotelian approach, i.e., from a
psychologistic or biologistic approach. It is all too understandable that physicists
react so allergically to Reich, as if he were in the truest sense of the word
"medieval."

Charles Konia argues in his book Neither Left Nor Right that all significant
political thinkers since antiquity adhered to either mystical or mechanistic theories
and tried to understand society on this inappropriate basis. Aristotle was said to be
the only exception, for he was first and foremost a natural scientist. Theory had to
follow facts. Moreover, he was practically the only one "with an understanding of
social organization grounded in biology" (Konia 2012, p. 6).

According to Konia, reality for Aristotle was not static but develops toward a goal,
i.e., it is functional in nature.3 For Aristotle, the whole (e.g., the state) takes
precedence over the part (the individual). The state is not composed of a number
of people (quantity), but of different kinds of people. The state is an organic
entity that regulates itself, not a machine to be used by power elites.
Accordingly, there should be not a

In particular, Konia emphasizes the difference from Plato's mysticism. How this
relates to the discovery of orgone can perhaps best be illustrated by the mind-body
problem. Hans Hass explains that for Plato, the body was only the instrument of
the soul. The body was a "vehicle" of the soul, which the soul steered like a
"helmsman."

[A]nd differently Aristotle and many after him saw the soul. It is

The proximity to orgone energy is evident! But let us continue with the critique of
Plato. The "flowing" in Heraclitus's sense, and thus time, had always been a
problem in the post-Galilean, so to speak "anti-Aristotelian" and deeply Platonic
physics, a problem that was finally resolved by Einstein: in four-dimensional
relativistic "spacetime," time was pure illusion. This changed in the 1920s, when
both quantum physicists, such as Heisenberg, and Reich ("I was for a time
considered a 'crazy Bergsonian,'" Reich 1942) were strongly influenced by the
philosophy of life. But let us consider some statements of the life-philosophers
(Nietzsche, Dilthey, Bergson) more closely:

Nietzsche: "There is no 'being' behind the doing and acting, becoming (…) the
doing is everything." And "Thus Heraclitus will be eternally right that being is an
empty fiction." About the opponent of Heraclitus, namely Parmenides and thus
Plato, Wilhelm Dilthey said: "True Platonist! who first fixes the becoming, flowing
things in concepts and then places the concept of flowing alongside as a
supplement."

In contrast, orgonomy, entirely in Dilthey's spirit, is about first understanding
nature, i.e., inwardly retracing it. That flowing requires a thing that moves is
already a secondary achievement of the intellect, a conceptual model that logically
attaches itself to the obstruction of flow, i.e., to armoring! At the beginning of the
18th century, George Berkeley wrote in his treatise on the principles of human
knowledge:

Despite his denial of the real world, Berkeley grasped 300 years ago the
functional identity between "thing-like" thinking and human armoring. In Ether,
God and Devil, Reich writes that "functional thinking tolerates no static states,
unless it has been subjected to the freezing of armoring. 'Flowing' is also nature
in every single one of its variant functions, as in the whole. Nature too knows no
static states" (Reich 1949a).


---


In his book Time Reborn, theoretical physicist Lee Smolin challenges the
Platonic/Einsteinian dictum that time is an illusion, and he arrives at an argument
that is largely identical to Reich's (Moskowitz 2013). Smolin: "Time is decisive,
and the experience of reality that we all have of being in the present is not an
illusion, but the deepest insight into the fundamental character of reality."

Initially, like most of his colleagues, he had assumed that time was merely
subjective, an illusion. But "over time" he became convinced that time is
something real and could even be the key to understanding the laws of nature.
These, after all, are not something absolute and unchangeable, quasi-Platonic
ideas, but would develop "over time."

Reflections on the nature of time are anything but abstract quibbles of
theoretical physicists. Smolin:

If I believe that the future is already determined, then
                     the things that constitute the most valuable part of
                     being human are, exactly like time itself, an illusion.
                     We strive for decisions in life. That is a valuable part
                     of our humanity. If the metaphysical picture that
                     corresponds to reality is that there are only atoms
                     moving in the void, then there will never be anything
                     new and nothing will ever be a surprise — it is only the
                     rearrangement of atoms. This goes hand in hand with a
                     loss of responsibility, as well as a loss of human dignity.

Every physics student has two things driven out of them from the outset: trust in
their natural sense of logic and trust in their own senses. Only with the help of
this Platonic didactics, and a well-organized inquisition, could the theory of
relativity survive. The reasons why Einstein was able to prevail (with all the
unimaginable ecological, economic, and social consequences that the destruction
of the physical sciences brought about) have nothing to do with physics, but are
attributable to the irrational human character structure. The only function of the
theory of relativity was to transform the universe into a lifeless desert of
mathematical formulas. Or as Reich put it:


---


Einstein succeeded in impressing the first half of the
                     twentieth century, precisely because he had emptied
                     space. To empty space in such a way that the whole
                     universe became a static nothing was the only theory
                     that could satisfy the desert-like character structure of

Smolin's colleagues, who are almost uniformly Platonically oriented, have what
appears to be a telling counter-argument against him: the meta-law dilemma. If
physical laws are subject to time, there must be a "meta-law" beyond time that
governs this development. In other words: the physical laws are ultimately still
above time!

Here the whole misery of the mechano-mystical worldview reveals itself, i.e., a
worldview that is a bizarre combination of "atoms moving in the void" and
Platonic philosophy, in which everything is governed by supernatural static ideas
(laws). This view of the world is a direct expression of the self-experience of the
armored organism.

As for the said "dilemma," it disappears with the discovery of orgone, because
with it a physical domain beyond "atoms moving in the void" was discovered,
one that breaks open the mechano-mystical worldview.

1.b. Orgonomic Functionalism
At the beginning of his career, Reich was a psychoanalyst, and his first
methodological reflections revolved around the already virulent criticism in the
1920s that psychoanalysis was not a science because it dealt only with subjective
phenomena. He countered that anyone who applied psychoanalysis correctly
would arrive at the same result, i.e., that objective processes were being inferred.
Through the application of his specific, i.e., systematically conducted type of
psychoanalysis, "character analysis," Reich eventually encountered something
regularly that he called "vegetative streaming" in reference to Friedrich Kraus
(Kraus 1927, Reich 1949b). It was the starting point of his investigation of nature.
First, in 1935, he tried to objectify these subjective streams through "bio-electric"
measurements, i.e., potential differences between an abraded "neutral" spot
immediately below the skin and the skin surface at the erogenous zones. During
pleasure, there was not only subjectively but, as the electrical potential changes
showed, also objectively an expansion; during anxiety, correspondingly, a
contraction.

Then Reich tried to observe the "vegetative streaming" directly under the
microscope, for example in transparent worms and single-celled organisms.
Through this unusual kind of extensive observation, he succeeded via the detour
of discovering what he called "bions" in ultimately the "discovery of orgone," i.e.,
that which streams through the body. The development of the "orgone
accumulator" then led him further to the investigation of "orgone energy" in the
atmosphere, which finally culminated in the "cloudbuster," which to this day is
used for weather modification by redirecting "atmospheric streams." With the help
of the miniature version of the cloudbuster, the Medical DOR-Buster, anyone can
experience what vegetative streaming is by slowly moving the tubes of the device
up and down, for example, on their leg.

This development would have been impossible if Reich had not taken subjective
sensations as a guide. He could rely on them because with his concept of
"armoring" developed in character analysis, he had a criterion for what in
subjectivity is trustworthy and what is not, i.e., what leads forward and what only
leads into a dead end. This is, for example, the subject of his book Ether, God
and Devil: that these three conceptual domains, "ether," "God," and "the Devil,"
each cover parts of what he himself discovered, but in a distorted way. The
"ether" (and everything that developed from it in quantum physics and relativity
theory after the 19th century) is a mechanistic distortion, and "God" is a mystical
one. The "Devil" is the perception of armoring itself, or of the drives that are
distorted by it ("secondary drives") (Reich 1949a).


---


In almost all descriptions of Reich's theories, as already hinted in section 1.a., it is
presented as if he had, so to speak, designed a comprehensive "worldview" that
largely corresponds to the 19th-century ether concept or the "living universe" of
various religions. These summaries are themselves an enormous distortion of
Reich's life's work. In fact, Reich only discovered that the same energetic
functions occur on the most diverse levels (single-celled organisms, the human
body, human society, the atmosphere, and finally galaxies), especially expansion,
contraction, "spiral wave movement," superimposition, radiation, and attraction.

In his research, he always looked for the common energetic functions that
connected the most diverse domains. From the outside, it may have looked as if
he was dabbling in the most diverse fields,5 but in fact he always stayed within his
own limited area of expertise: the energetic functions that he subsumed under the
concept of "orgone."

The concept of "function" can only be explained through concrete examples. For
instance, the spiky coat of a hedgehog and the works council of a company have
roughly the same function, yet in terms of their development and material
structures, absolutely nothing connects the two domains. They only share the
same function. In "energetic functionalism" or "orgonomic functionalism," it is
specifically about how orgone energy manifests in the most diverse domains and
on the most diverse scales. Orgone energy has certain characteristic properties: it
moves spontaneously from within itself, pulsates, moves in spiral waves, is
anentropic, radiates, etc.

In the 1970s, "Reichians" attempted to replace functionalism with structuralism
(which in ethnology, for example, had entirely yielded to structuralism). The
world is conceived as a kind of stencil that first gives meaning to the objects that
fill it — or something like that. Whether this barely comprehensible "theory"
better conveys what Reich wanted to express, I strongly doubt. Moreover,
functionalism arose from field research, while structuralism was an armchair
invention. Structuralism is about a superordinate system of structures; an

of values, and fatally recalls the Platonic basic structure of school physics! One

1.c. Anti-functionalism: The Mind in the Machine
The difference between mechano-mysticism and functionalism is self-movement.
Unarmored life functions from within itself, i.e., movement is intrinsic to it,
structure is secondary. Primitive animism comes quite close to this. In armored
life, structure (and consciousness) takes center stage. It is rigid (armoring) and
must therefore be set in motion and controlled by "higher centers" (the said
consciousness). This is the unity of mechanism and mysticism. They are
inseparable from each other, just as the machine is dead until it is set in motion
by humans. It always needs, freely after Kant, an impulse from the sphere of
"reason" characterized by free will: ultimately the conscience, "the judge in us,"
God, the superego — armoring = "the Absolute," the absolutely static, from which
alone movement can proceed. Reich elaborated extensively on this in Ether, God
and Devil (Reich 1949a).

At the beginning of the Enlightenment, Kant relied on reason to morally control
the animal drives, while at its end Freud emphasized the importance of drives in
order to make them conscious — and thereby better subjugate them to reason.
Despite first appearances, there is therefore no contradiction whatsoever
between the two thinkers. One might just as well try to pit Kant's emphasis on
reason against C.G. Jung's anti-Enlightenment "ghost-seeing," but in fact, by his
own admission, the central elements of Kantian philosophy — the intangible "thing
in itself" and the categorical imperative — shaped Jung's thinking about
"archetypes" and the "collective unconscious." Jung was, despite all his criticism
of the cult of reason, a confessed adherent of Kant's philosophical criticism.
Obviously, a common functional principle runs through these supposed
oppositions!

Kant argued against materialistic determinism in order to open a space, the
"thing in itself," for "free will," i.e., to save religion and morality. The will is free
when it gives laws to itself. Kant provided it with the necessary space. Jung used
this approach to open the floodgates, in a seemingly legitimate way, for his
personal, to paraphrase Freud, "mud floods of the occult." With Freud, quite the
embodiment of reason itself, the unconscious, for example every slip of the
tongue, was completely "mechanically" determined. In Freudian psychoanalysis,
there should therefore be no place for religion and morality per se, but in fact
Freud, entirely in Schopenhauer's spirit, was solely concerned with denying, or
"condemning" in Freudian fashion, the ultimately frustration- and death-bringing
"will to life," which after all is only a "death drive." Here too the human is to
become "free"!


---


Kant was concerned with absolute moral rules ("categorical imperative"),
accordingly he turned vehemently and explicitly against the functional
perspective, i.e., the alignment of actions according to their probable
consequences. In a similar way, but on the basis of an ontological determinism,
Freud wanted to control everything "morally," to eradicate all spontaneity, just as
a mechanist controls a machine. The intellect was to gain "the dictatorship in
human mental life" through psychoanalysis, just as the

www.orgonomie.net
                                         14

engineer becomes dictator of the machine when he analyzes its construction and
wiring plan and thereby eliminates the last remnant of apparent "indeterminism."
Into the jungle of the unconscious, paths of consciousness were to be cut, and
thus the uncivilized interior colonized. Jung, on the other hand, restored the
medieval moral order with the help of the collective unconscious and its
archetypes (God and his angels). Freud, quite the (pseudo-) Enlightenment
thinker, replaced God and the angels with reason (making the unconscious
conscious, replacing the id with the ego). In principle, despite all superficial
incompatibility, they were in agreement!

Both, the rationalist Freud and the irrationalist Jung, directed themselves against
spontaneous functioning, i.e., they were born enemies of Reich. It fits the picture
that the early psychoanalyst Otto Gross, influenced not least by Max Stirner, was
diagnosed as "schizophrenic" by Jung and ultimately eliminated in collaboration
with Freud, "to save morality." The drives had to be tamed, and anyone who
"denounced" this taming as the fons et origo of illness and baseness had to be
expelled from the tribe as "schizophrenic," to perish in the hostile wilderness. All
just as was later done to Reich (Laska 2024). Ultimately, Kant was upheld, i.e.,
one should not behave as a "drive-governed" individual, but always as all should
act, i.e., "reason-governed." One might also say that the drives were
"collectivized" from the start, i.e., the "archetypes" are "forms of intuition" that
declare a patriarchal, reactionary, armored feeling and thinking a priori to be
unchangeable "nature."


---


Whether in the name of Enlightenment or counter-Enlightenment, of determinism
or indeterminism, of science or ghost-seeing, i.e., whatever incompatible way of
thinking — it is always and exclusively about saving the superego and further
implementing it. Without exception, all philosophy and psychology revolve around
nothing else. "Culture comes first." And that is exactly the original root of all this.
As I will demonstrate in the next section, Kant's entire epistemological endeavor
goes back to saving morality (the superego, armoring) from LaMettrie's
materialist critique of morality. LaMettrie, Stirner, Gross, Reich on the one side,
Kant, Hegel, Freud, Jung on the other.

1.d. Kant, Swedenborg, Newton and LaMettrie
I use the term "biophilosophy" here in the same sense that Reich spoke of
"biopsychology" and "biosociology." The humanities are viewed from a certain
perspective, that of the biophysical structure of the human being. Keywords are:
organismic orgone energy, three-layer model, orgonotic system, energetic
orgonom, radiation, attraction, excitation, perception, orgonotic streaming, etc.
Mostly it is simply a matter of understanding the human, here the "philosophizing
human," from the perspective of their armoring.

In the late 1940s, Reich, as a decidedly non-philosopher, briefly engaged with
Kant's "thing in itself" in Ether, God and Devil (Reich 1949a). A concept he knew
from the work History of Materialism (Lange 1866) by the Neo-Kantian F.A. Lange.
He had read the book during his student days and drew on it throughout his life.
Lange translates Kant's abstract a priori of transcendental forms of intuition and
categories into the world of experience itself. With the postulation of a separate
"world of reason," Kant wanted to defend Newton and atomism against Hume's
skepticism while simultaneously saving free will and thus morality. Lange, on the
other hand, undermines materialism through — materialist (namely
"physiological") arguments and simultaneously saves ethics — materialistically:
humans think beyond the purely material in order to secure their material
interests.


---


Kant had, in the name of the Enlightenment, tried to put a stop to ghost-seeing,
particularly that of the "seer of spirits" Immanuel Swedenborg, by, in Lange's
reading, so to speak "biophilosophically" recursing to the structure of the human
senses. We do not perceive the world as it is, but according to the construction of
our perceptual organs. This deprives any metaphysics that wants to look behind
the veils of reality at a supposedly "more real" level, "the spiritual world," of its
ground. Swedenborg was not propagating "higher truths," but empty concepts and
thus simply nonsense. Kant's critique of any metaphysics naturally also affected
atoms, for they too were merely an inaccessible and therefore void "thing in
itself." Nevertheless, their postulation, according to Kant, was a rationally
necessary assumption.

Reich, on the other hand, defends against Kant the so-called "correspondence
theory of truth": truth is contact with reality (i.e., contact with the "thing in itself").
He does this because the body with which we perceive is "armored" and
correspondingly can be "de-armored": from a distorted perception, an undistorted
one emerges. It is not, as in Kantian "transcendental idealism," the subject here
and completely separately the object there, but both are connected through the
concept of "biophysical structure." The substrate of this connection is the "orgone
energy continuum," which provides access to the "thing in itself."7

7

they have survived over countless generations. "If the key fits, it reproduces the lock 1:1!"

One cannot carry deterministic materialism (for Lange ultimately synonymous with
"atomism") to its conclusion, because consciousness, "life," society are simply not
explainable in this way. No matter how complicated one builds a machine from
"building blocks" (atoms), it may behave, but it will never act consciously.8
There must accordingly be something "third" ("the thing in itself") between
"atom A" and "atom B" to make the living and "the mind" thinkable at all. In this
way, Lange, ultimately Kant, pointed the "biophilosophical" way from
mechanistic (atomistic) materialism to orgonomic functionalism.

Through Christian Fernandes' dissertation on LaMettrie, I came across a third
aspect of Kant's "biophilosophy" already addressed in section 1.c. I quote:

2. Orgone Biophysics
2.a. Theory of Science

If things were really done scientifically, a large proportion of medications would have to be taken off the market, and a large proportion of physicians would have serious problems, because they constantly make untenable diagnoses and propose senseless therapies. This is so because medicine hangs, as it were, helplessly in the air. It is not a science at all; no fundamental concepts underlie it — rather, it is an eternal, wretched cobbling-together of patches.

One may object that orgonomy is the least suited of all things to serve as such a fundamental theory for medicine, since its basic assumptions are so "metaphysical" that they can be neither empirically verified nor falsified. That reminds me of the theory of relativity, which is likewise quite questionable in this respect, since it explains material events by means of changes in the "metaphysical" space-time framework. Accordingly, it is indeed claimed again and again that there are no experiments that prove the theory of relativity. On this, Hubert Goenner, Professor of Theoretical Physics in Göttingen, says:

From the various kinds of experiments described, with which the Special Theory of Relativity was checked and confirmed with high accuracy as a theory correctly describing natural processes, we see (…) that talk of the experimentum crucis, as a single all-decisive experiment, is as a rule out of place in physics. Before a theory is recognized, a whole network of mutually supporting observations and experiments testing the theory in various ways must have been built up. (Goenner 2005, p. 49)

Reading Reich's writings, for example concerning contact-electrical phenomena or the work with the Geiger counter, is sometimes an imposition. The physically trained reader — i.e. the "physical ex-layman," who laboriously learned correct physical thinking from textbooks — understandably directs all his frustration and anger at the "physical layman" Reich, who evidently did not submit himself to this torture. The composed expert, by contrast — Einstein, for instance, in his discussion with Reich — overlooks such purely craft-level deficiencies, since he is primarily interested in new proposed solutions to fundamental problems and in fundamentally new observations. From Reich's diary entries and letters from the 1930s it becomes apparent how, already in his bioelectrical and bion experiments, he had to struggle with small-minded experts who wanted to "control" everything until every phenomenon that did not fit their textbook theories had truly been "measured away" (Reich 1997).


---


As for the reproducibility of Reich's findings: one cannot botch together a "control experiment" on the side; rather, one must really get down on one's knees into the

www.orgonomie.net
                                         20

problems, work one's way into the technical difficulties, and one fundamental prerequisite for this is a certain measure of respect, or at least scientific curiosity. What would an investigation of the temperature difference postulated by Reich between the orgone energy accumulator and a thermodynamically identical control box yield, if this experiment were carried out in foggy November, or in an energetically contaminated physics laboratory?!

In real science (and there is no "more scientific" science than physics!) things simply do not proceed the way Karl Popper and his echoers, for instance the proponents of "Evidence Based Medicine," want to make us believe! The Popperian dictum that research is about falsifying, not verifying, is a brainchild conceived in the abstract, for in this way not only could orgonomy never have developed — hardly anything in science could have. How in the world does one intend to "falsify" a fundamental theory like the theory of relativity, or the orgone theory, or quantum theory?! The only question can be whether the "network" described by Goenner holds or does not hold, and how large the holes in the fabric of the network are!

From an abstract-logical standpoint, the demand for falsifiability is perfectly comprehensible, yet it can only rarely be fulfilled. How is one to falsify the orgone energy hypothesis, when objections of one kind or another can always be raised? Air humidity too high, an "energetically dead environment," etc. A corresponding example is the extremely elaborate neutrino research: the experimental setups must be placed in deep mines in order truly to shield out every (other) cosmic radiation; nevertheless, the radioactive radiation of the surrounding rock remains, the complicated experimental setup is in itself quite ambivalent (flashes of light are measured and neutrinos are inferred from them), and the neutrinos themselves could convert, on their way, into one of the two kinds of neutrinos that cannot be measured. It is practically impossible to want to "falsify" anything here, since, for example, the problem of "too few solar neutrinos" can mean anything at all. In the real, "dirty" world, scientific theories are rarely "falsified"; rather, they simply die out because alternative theories are more attractive.


---


Or take the neutrons. How do we actually know that they exist? They occur neither in nature — if one disregards the insignificant release of neutrons by cosmic radiation — nor can one see or feel them when they leave the atomic pile. Nevertheless, we are (rightly) firmly convinced that neutrons and the rest of the particle zoo exist. But of all things, with the orgone — which one can feel and see — doubts surface, because it does not fit into the edifice of theories, i.e. of opinions, of experts who have never engaged with the subject and therefore are not experts at all.

Alongside the orgone theory, many other scientific theories are also difficult to falsify. I am thinking, for example, of the theory of evolution, in which all manner of intermediate forms between species are missing, in which the transition from one species to another has never been observed or experimentally verified, etc. A scientific theory slowly perishes, like a fire that receives no new nourishment and

www.orgonomie.net
                                         21

flares up anew with every new successful experiment and every confirming observation. In this respect, the orgone theory does not differ in anything from other scientific theories.

In seminars on the theory of science the world admittedly looks somewhat different, and I regard these approaches too as an important corrective; but in reality, the scientific world is a battlefield. It is like medications that disappear from the market, not because they were "falsified" as ineffective, but because they go out of fashion.

In general, correct theories are, at the outset, so weak in the face of the "established scientific opinion" that science could not develop at all if one immediately subjected these theories to a reign of falsification-terror. For example, the Copernican system at first did not agree with the observational data any better — rather, it agreed worse than the old worldview. If one had proceeded "scientifically," we would still believe that the sun revolves around the earth!

It is a mere myth that science develops linearly and in the rhythm of falsifiable hypotheses. In fact, it develops evolutionarily: in different strands that continue to fan out further, of which, however, most strands die off again. And less because they were falsified than because their scientific following has died out and has not found enough successors. Whether this process correlates with the truth, one can only hope. One need only recall how Lamarckism, after many deaths, supposedly finally expired once and for all by the 1960s at the latest — only to celebrate a triumphant resurrection today in the form of epigenetics.


---


It may well be that, by the understanding of science held by a pharmacologist, orgonomic medicine has "the same status as foot reflexology" — but from a genuinely scientific point of view it constitutes an impressively tightly knit, forgive the expression, "Goennerian network"!

Any responsible orgonomist will immediately send his patient to a bowel specialist if anything twinges or twacks in the little belly! But he would be rightly nonplussed if the bowel specialist, from his mole's-eye perspective, were to presume to pronounce on the comprehensive function that the bowel symptom has within the overall structure of the disease process.

2.b. Wilhelm Reich: Physician and Physicist
With his discovery of orgone energy, Dr. med. Wilhelm Reich stands in a continuous tradition of physicians who have pointed physics in new directions. This approach was extremely fruitful; the reverse one — drawing conclusions from inanimate nature about animate nature — has brought us mechanistic genetics. Here is the tradition in which Reich stands:

The physician Georg Bauer, alias Agricola (1494–1555), founded the study of rocks and mining. As the greatest authority on the subject of magnetism in his time and as the founder of the experimental method, the physician William Gilbert (1540–1603) distinguished himself. The term "electric" comes from him. His professional colleague Santoro Santorio (1561–1636), founder of the scientific school of thought in medicine, who also constructed a medical balance scale for studying metabolism, not only measured fever with a thermometer for the first time but also invented the hygrometer. The physician and "iatrochemist" Johann Baptist Helmont (1577–1644) was the first to distinguish other gases from the "element air." James Hutton (1726–1797), likewise a physician, was the founder of geology. The professor of medicine Joseph Black (1728–1799) discovered specific heat and heat of transformation.

The professor of anatomy Luigi Galvani (1737–1798) helped to found the modern theory of electricity. Bizzi and Chiurco, two collaborators of Walter Hoppe (who brought orgonomy to Germany at the beginning of the 1970s), write of Galvani's research that with it he was the first to approach life energy experimentally. Although he is recognized as the founder of electrophysiology, he in reality founded a theory of life energy. He first called the biological energy he discovered "animal electricity," then "galvanic fluid," and finally "life force."11 Galvani even went so far as to postulate a connection between atmospheric electricity — the "electric" ocean — and the organism. The authors connect this point with the concept of Benjamin Franklin (1706–1790), who explained electrostatic phenomena by means of a pulsating "uniform fluid" (Hoppe 1984).


---


The physician Thomas Young (1773–1829) arrived, through his work on the physiological optics of the eye, at the revival of Huygens's wave theory. Another physician, William Prout (1785–1850), put forward the hypothesis — so fruitful for the development of chemistry and physics, and named after him — that the atoms of the elements consist of multiples of the hydrogen atom. Ernst Heinrich Weber (1795–1878), a professor of anatomy and physiology, together with his brother, the professor of physics Wilhelm Edward Weber (1804–1891), founded wave theory experimentally. They made the first observations on the difference between group velocity and wave velocity. The famous Léon Foucault (1819–1868) was a physician by training. With his pendulum experiments he demonstrated the rotation of the Earth's axis experimentally, measured the

11
     A striking parallel to the history of the term "orgone energy."

www.orgonomie.net
                                         23

speed of light, and worked on the induced electro-magnetic "Foucault" eddy currents.

Julius Robert Mayer (1814–1878) was the first to formulate the general law of the conservation of energy. Through observations in his medical practice he had arrived at the conclusion that mechanical energy, heat, and chemical energy are equivalent. Working in the same field and in the same direction — from biology toward physics — was the professor of physiology Hermann von Helmholtz (1821–1894), who later taught physics. He invented instruments for examining the eyes, founded the physical theory of the sensation of tone, studied the propagation speed of nerve excitation, and put forward, among other things, the hypothesis of the atomic nature of electricity. The physiologist Henry Gray (1825–1861) distinguished between conductors and non-conductors of electricity.

Reich had come to medicine through his pronounced interests in the natural sciences, and here above all to sexology. Inevitably, then, he had to encounter Freud. Freud's theories emerged from his neurological research, from Darwinian biology (e.g., ontogeny as the repetition of phylogeny), or for example from the "psychophysics" of Gustav Theodor Fechner (1801–1887), who in turn, as a student of Schelling, goes back to German natural philosophy.


---


Today people like to act as though Freud used the term "energy" (originally a biological term) merely as a pure, quasi-"poetic" metaphor; but for him it was rather an explanatory construct of natural science. Reich then showed what reality corresponded to this construct. But while Freud wanted to emancipate himself from his background as a physiologist, Reich carried the original natural-scientific approach further, came to biology, and finally — like many physicians before him — to physics, founding in the process a new natural-scientific edifice similar to that of the physician Franz Anton Mesmer (1734–1815). The systems of both men ranged from questions of medicine, or of education for example, all the way to physical considerations of electricity and gravitation. There is also a direct line from Mesmer to Reich, for Mesmer's pupil Puysegur invented the hypnosis that was practiced by Freud's teacher Charcot.

Apart from through the Danish physicist and Schelling student Hans Christian Oersted (1777–1851), German natural philosophy had almost no influence on physics. One exception is the influence — already mentioned in section 1.a. — of "German philosophy of life" on people like Heisenberg in the formulation of quantum mechanics. Natural philosophy had led Oersted to seek unity in nature. Thus he built the bridge between electricity and magnetism. Natural philosophy had a decisive influence on biology (e.g., on cell theory and embryology). With Reich, an offshoot of natural philosophy (mediated through Bergson, Freud, and others) with its principal characteristics — the life-energy concept and, in the broadest sense, a "dialectical" way of looking at things — was finally to help physics toward concrete discoveries as well, after Goethe had failed with his theory of color and could operate only in the biological domain of "subjective colors."

Similarly, Reich did not accept the laws of nature as given, static, and unalterable, but sought to fathom their genesis, to ground them in orgone energy functions.

2.d. Cosmic Radiation
Modern physics can explain everything — truly everything — that surrounds us with
quantum electrodynamics, i.e. the interaction between electrons and
photons. Only the strong force — the force that holds the
atomic nucleus together — and the General Theory of Relativity fall outside this frame. These two
areas stand, so to speak, outside the door, because each concerns a special problem:
the extreme concentration of matter into a (relatively) extremely small space. How to explain that atomic nuclei can exist at all, in which positively charged protons repel one another at this distance with a force that is simply infinite? And what
happens when astronomical accumulations of mass collapse under their own
gravity, thereby attracting one another with a force that grows steadily toward infinity?

For the "everyday phenomena" mentioned at the outset, orgonomy has its own
explanations; but what explanation does it have for the strong force that holds atomic nuclei
together, and for "black holes" that suck everything in their vicinity into a timeless
singularity? Reich showed that electrical phenomena
(mechanical potential) can be reduced to orgone energy (orgonomic
potential), and that observations of "charge separation" in the laboratory therefore cannot simply
be extrapolated to regions that belong to another, deeper
functional domain. Something similar can be said about "black holes,"
which are, first of all, merely a theoretical construct, i.e. they cannot be directly observed.
What one sees are jets, i.e. bundled beams of matter emitted by
supermassive "black holes" located at the centers of
galaxies. What we are observing is the conversion of superimposed
currents of cosmic orgone energy (orgonomic potential) into linearly
radiating secondary energy and matter (mechanical potential). Something similar
happens in the radioactive decay of atomic nuclei. When protons collide in
particle accelerators, "jets" — bundles of particles — are produced.

"Cosmic radiation," as we know today, is in fact a
particle radiation. It was discovered in 1912. Reich was uncertain what
relation orgone energy had to it. On the one hand they could not be identical,
since no one had yet seen cosmic rays with the naked eye; moreover,
these were supposed to originate from distant outer space, whereas Reich had discovered "orgone radiation"
in bion preparations in the laboratory. If the cosmic
rays were of planetary origin, however, "then they would be the same as the
orgone rays. The assumed great 'penetrating power' of the 'cosmic
rays' would then be explained simply by the fact that orgone is present
everywhere" (Reich 1948, p. 113).

Reich had every reason to connect this extremely strong radiation with orgone energy,
all the more so since at that time the processes that could have generated such
monstrous energies in the universe were not even conceivable in
outline.


---


After his death, i.e., since the 1960s, astrophysicists suspected that the remnants of supernovae could be responsible for the "galactic cosmic radiation" by causing shock waves in the interstellar gas, which in turn produce magnetic fields that accelerate protons, alpha particles, and heavier atomic nuclei such that they become cosmic radiation.

For the particularly energy-rich ("extragalactic") particles of cosmic radiation, only two sources come into question: particle acceleration in the vicinity of black holes or gamma-ray explosions. In these gamma-ray bursts, neutrinos would also have to be produced (at least according to the physicists' previous model concepts). However, to this day no measurements are available that confirm this. Cosmic radiation remains a mystery even a century after its discovery.

Charles Konia addressed in his essay "Visualization of Cosmic Rays" the possibility that cosmic radiation could in fact be nothing other than—orgone energy (Konia 1972b).

He refers to observations by the crew of Apollo 11, who perhaps once per minute saw intense light flashes (light points, light rays, and a few "double light points"). These light flashes were attributed to cosmic radiation because corresponding particle tracks were found in the plastic helmets that the astronauts wore.

The author of the Science article to which Konia refers, G.L. Wick, also points out corresponding visual perceptions near accelerators. But not least, to the remnants of cosmic radiation at sea level—the muons. To investigate this, Wick had test subjects who had sat in absolute darkness for at least 30 minutes describe their visual impressions. At the same time, detectors for muons (scintillators) were placed both below and above them. Obviously, the test subjects were able to perceive light flashes in connection with cosmic radiation!

Konia quotes from Reich's The Cancer, where he describes what happens in a darkened room lined with metal (i.e., a Faraday cage) as follows:


---


In the course of about half an hour, the black gives way to an indeterminate (blue-gray, PN) shimmer. Our eyes are irritated by strange light phenomena. It is as if nebulous swaths of gray-blue color were moving slowly through the room. When we stare at a certain point on the wall, we see moving light phenomena. The longer we remain in the room, the more distinct the light phenomena become. Within the gray-blue swaths, we perceive deep blue-violet points of light. 2.e. Mechanistic Equivalents to the Orgone Energy Accumulator

Originally, "heat" was regarded as a kind of "fluid," which is also plausible. One feels how heat "escapes" from one outdoors and "flows toward" one at the stove. This is roughly how people imagined things when thermodynamics was formulated at the beginning of the 19th century. Eventually, however, it was recognized that heat is not a kind of "thing," but rather a "mode of behavior" of the smallest, more or less freely movable units of matter—their chaotic, undirected "trembling motion."

However, this gave rise to a significant problem: Thermodynamics had shown that, as long as the experimental setup is left to itself, heat equalization always occurs. If I carefully pour hot water into a container with cold water, for example, so that a "hot water zone" forms, sooner or later the entire water will assume the same temperature, which (in a closed system) depending on the ratio of quantities, lies somewhere between the two original water temperatures.


---


That this must be so can be visualized: if one regards water as an accumulation of molecules that fly wildly back and forth, up and down, and collide in the process—very quickly all molecules will adopt the same pattern of motion. The picture changes only when I consider individual molecules: on average they may all move equally fast and equally disordered, yet the individual molecule A moves less in a given time period than molecule B.

One could now separate the somewhat slower A-molecules individually from the somewhat faster B-molecules—and would again have a hot and a cold water zone before one. In this way, the Second Law of Thermodynamics, which states that isolated systems always strive for temperature equalization, would be rendered absurd.

In the mid-19th century, James Clerk Maxwell played through precisely this thought experiment: small "demons" could turn thermodynamics on its head by always opening a barrier between two zones when a fast molecule threatens to slam against it, while keeping the barrier closed for a slow molecule. It would be something like the coffee in the cup getting hot all by itself! Not from nothing, but from information about the behavior of individual molecules, energy would arise.

After 150 years, the Japanese researcher Shoichi Toyabe from Chuo University, Tokyo, was actually able to carry out such an experiment in reality. Exactly how he did this need not concern us further here (Toyabe 2010). The problem with the whole matter is, of course, that we are not dealing with a kind of "spirit being" ("Maxwell's demons"), but with Toyabe and the very elaborate and energy-intensive apparatus with which he identifies and manipulates the individual molecules. An "energy gain" cannot be achieved this way!

On the other hand, Toyabe did not touch the molecules either, i.e., he did not directly transfer energy to them, but merely erected barriers (in his case, electric fields) or not, depending on the situation. This is something fundamentally different from heating one zone and cooling the other zone by adding or removing heat energy!

This experiment is of interest because it represents the mechanistic equivalent of the orgone energy accumulator. If one takes the orgonomic potential seriously, then "Maxwell's demons" must actually exist. Certainly not in the form of little "Toyabes," but as a counterpart to that life energy that makes Toyabe alive and enables him to conduct his experiment.


---


The fundamental question arises regarding the nature of thermal motion, roughly like the question of whether "heat waves" really only go back to differently warmed zones of the air or whether atmospheric orgone energy also plays a role. Undoubtedly, thermal motion exists, but even the discoverer of chaotic molecular motion, the so-called "Brownian motion," Robert Brown, was originally convinced that he was dealing with living (quasi "life-energetic") processes: living motion at the smallest possible level, which ultimately culminates in the movement of living beings (Jones 2013).

2.f. Thermal Orgonometry

Among others, Courtney F. Baker (under the pseudonym C. Frederick Rosenblum) and Charles Konia have experimentally addressed the thermal effects of orgone energy (Baker CF 1972, Konia 1972a).

In the orgone energy accumulator (ORAC), as the name suggests, a higher concentration of orgone energy spontaneously occurs (To-T). This can be demonstrated beyond any reasonable doubt by constructing a second container next to the ORAC, the control unit, which possesses the same thermodynamic properties, i.e., cools down or warms up just as fast as the ORAC. This can be accomplished relatively simply through estimation and subsequent empirical fine-calibration. If both containers, the ORAC and the control unit, warm up and cool down again just as fast, but the ORAC is continuously warmer in good weather, while in bad weather the temperature difference equalizes and perhaps even becomes negative—then conventional physics can only take refuge in silly objections and talk down the experimental setup until every chance is truly taken away for the orgone to show itself.15

James DeMeo has taken up all possible objections that have been raised since the 1940s and 1970s against Reich, Baker, Konia et al., and addressed them in his "definitive" To-T experiment: the temperature difference discovered by Reich is definitely not reducible to school-physics mechanisms (DeMeo 2009).

Even more devastating for the foundations of mechanistic physics are Konia's experiments with the DOR-buster. This is a miniature version of Reich's cloudbuster: metal pipes are "grounded" in water and draw orgone energy from the zone toward which they are directed and conduct it into water. Konia's consideration was whether the water should not thereby become warmer corresponding to the ORAC (Td-T). Indeed, this is the case, but it turns out that the two devices function diametrically opposite. The following overview illustrates this:

From this one can see that orgone energy is not a mechanical energy, like electromagnetism, radioactivity, and gravitation, but the true life energy. It behaves spontaneously and is only partially calculable, quite in accordance with living beings.

2.i. Cosmic Superimposition

At the end of The Cancer Biopathy, in the section "The Plan 'Orgonon': On the Possibility of Cancer Prevention," Reich describes a kind of "people's orgonomy" (Reich 1948). Simple people know instinctively about bionous decay and orgone energy. In contrast to the degenerate scientists, they draw the right conclusions from their healthy folk intuition. Here is an example from astronomy:


---


---
name: chunk_0042.en.txt
description: English translation of chunk 42 from Wilhelm Reich Ether Physics and Orgone Experiments (orgonomie.net). Source: https://orgonomie.net/hdowrphysik.pdf. Language: German. Discusses Jupiter's cloud formations photographed by NASA's Juno probe, citizen science image processing, and similarities to Van Gogh's "Starry Night" illustrating Reich's cosmic superimposition concept.
---

Recently I wanted to demonstrate Reich's concept of cosmic superimposition using Jupiter's cloud formations, but quickly gave up because the raw images were not very convincing in this regard. Now this: "Citizen scientists" have played with the colors and contrast of these raw images on their home computers—and stumbled upon cosmic superimposition.

Jupiter's swirling cloud peaks, photographed by NASA's Juno probe, look like something from a dream or even like a famous painting. Citizen scientist Amelia Carolina Sparavigna saw the similarities between the snapshot from the real world and the swirling patterns with which Vincent van Gogh depicted the night sky in his famous painting "Starry Night."17 She replaced this section of the painting, and the resulting image shows the similarities between art and nature. (Sparavigna is not the only JunoCam user pointing out the similarity.)18 (Cofield 2017)

We have here an outstanding example of a kind of "citizen science" that coincidentally comes remarkably close to orgonomy:

The raw images that JunoCam sends back to Earth usually don't look like much, but they contain information that can be extracted with the help of image processing and color correction. The resulting images can highlight certain features in the cloud peaks or simulate how the planet might appear to the naked human eye from close range. Most of the work that went into processing the JunoCam images was done by citizen scientists.19 The JunoCam team places the raw image files online and anyone can take them, process them, and publish the final result on the JunoCam website. Some users go to extremes in processing and create amazing

17 It is part of orgonomy folklore that van Gogh's painting "Starry Night" illustrates Reich's concept of cosmic superimposition. See the video "Stary Night (animation)" https://youtu.be/Bm9YvhBUmc4
18 See Klotz 2016.
19 See Cofield 2016.

www.orgonomie.net

artworks,20 while others process the images in such a way that they reveal new details and thus help in further decoding the planet's secrets.

3. Wilhelm Reich as a Physical Layman
3.a. Electrons
Reich will have worked through the physics textbook for physicians, biologists, and psychologists of his physics professor at the University of Vienna, Prof. Dr. Ernst Lecher. The first sentences of the book are remarkable, where Lecher expounds that physics is "the study of the motion of non-living bodies." From the very beginning, the movements of our own body "and the subjective sensations of force triggered in many cases in the process" had led to this conception. However, there exists the possibility that one day a different worldview might emerge, after one had penetrated deeper into the nature of electricity. Perhaps then, "from an electrical point of view," all natural processes would be more comprehensible and understandable. In a footnote on the second page, Lecher expounds that all matter could be broken down into atoms, which themselves are "perhaps different arrangements of one and the same fundamental building block of everything that exists, the electron" (Lecher 1912).

This "research program" was shattered a few years later with the discovery of further elementary particles. Reich himself had to abandon his considerations about a "bioelectricity" at the end of the 1930s, but at the same time it becomes clear that Lecher was one of many who led Reich to the discovery of orgone. The mainstream of science naturally developed in the opposite direction and pushed Reich into the corner of the "pseudo-scientist"... However, "majority decisions" play no role in science, or rather they should play none! We can calmly wait to see who will stand there as the charlatan in the end, that is, who has understood the inner logic of nature and who has gone hopelessly astray.

Unfortunately, Reich's style of argumentation in his disputes with physicists like Einstein sometimes makes a rather naive and even amateurish impression. In order to appreciate his arguments adequately, it must be remembered that, according to Reich, orgonomy begins "completely from scratch,"

in a fundamentally independent manner and without falling back on the
                     theories of classical natural science.
                     Not because it wants to, but because it must. Its
                     starting point is neither the electron nor the atom, neither
                     a linear motion in empty space nor a world spirit or
                     an eternal value. Its starting point is the observable
                     and measurable functions in the cosmic orgone ocean, from
                     which everything that exists, the physical as well as the emotional,
                     emerges. (Reich 1951a, p. 22)

The student of orgonomy who wants to discuss Reich's contributions to physics with others quickly finds himself in an untenable position. Already by the middle of the last

www.orgonomie.net
                                         46


---


century, Reich's position (e.g., that matter is "destroyed" by boiling, autoclaving, and incineration, and that in the process, freely according to Einstein's energy-mass relation, "energy bubbles" are released, or that additional electrons float in ionized air) was so far behind the times (it roughly corresponded to the "energetic-monistic" worldview before 1900) that it is enough to make one tear one's hair out. Any physicist, indeed any high school student, in 1950 would have had to set aside The Cancer Biopathy (Reich 1948) with a shake of the head.

In dealing with Reichian physics, one must show a certain degree of sensitivity and grant Reich a high degree of credit, as the physicist (diploma) Lars Jörgenson did with his outstanding book An Overview of the Gray Zone in Science on the delicate question of static electricity (Jörgenson 1990). Any other physicist would have brushed aside Reich's work with a remark, for most of them are rather conceited, and every generation regards physical theory as, in principle, complete and therefore the introduction of a new energy as absurd from the outset. That was already the case in the century before last.

Physics is practically no longer practiced as a natural science today; rather, it has been degraded to a poorer kind of engineering, reduced to a handmaiden of technology, or has evaporated into the exploration of the Platonic world of ideas, searching for "symmetries" in particle accelerators that are just as expensive as they are pointless. There is no more physics, and anyone who, like Jörgenson or Reich, wants to pursue physics must either hide behind a pseudonym or reckon with the end of his career.

In 1946, Reich noted: "Very gradually and tentatively, it is becoming clear to me that I understand infinitely more about the real processes in nature than the conventional physicists do" (Reich 1999, p. 336).

Not by chance does orgone physics take up by far the largest space in Jörgenson's work (100 pages). This presentation, if one generously overlooks minor blunders (with the respect the author has earned for his achievement), is certainly the best (and, yes, probably so far also the only) overall presentation of orgonomic physics. This presentation, in connection with the explanation of parallel but completely independent research approaches, makes this book a jewel.

Through the other approaches to researching cosmic life energy presented by Jörgenson, one gains a completely new, "more comprehensive" feeling for orgonomy. Engaging with the other research approaches can help eliminate systematic errors, or better said "systematic blind spots," from orgonomy. Jörgenson is particularly good at showing functional, mutually complementary connections between the sometimes extremely different approaches, without forcing a harmonized overall view on the reader.


---


"The central question the book deals with is: Is there a second kind of electricity, and how does it act on various substances and living beings?" Thus, Reich's problematic engagement with static electricity is taken up again here in a very broad framework. For example, Carl Ludwig Reichenbach found that frictional electricity and battery electricity were completely disparate things for his sensitives. While static electricity produced the greatest "od light" of all, it was only slight with battery electricity, and in the battery elements themselves every odic effect was absent. Moreover, positive static electricity was od-negative, while the corresponding positive electrode of a battery was, confusingly, od-positive (Jörgenson 1990, pp. 13f). Fortin believed in a fluid in nature whose free motion is slowed when passing through matter, whereby the fluid transforms into an electrostatic charge, which in turn can be discharged in a balancing current (Jörgenson 1990, p. 69). Such approaches from the early period of electricity research must be taken up again by orgonomy in order to finally establish a satisfying connection between orgone energy, static electricity, and battery electricity.

Unfortunately, as indicated, Reich did not proceed carefully in exposing the "theories of classical natural science." For example, he attributed the theory of the two electrical "fluids" to the fictitious "electrophysicist" with whom he discusses in his essay "Orgonotic Pulsation" (Reich 1944). Friedrich Engels describes this theory in his Dialectics of Nature as follows:


---


Like heat and light, electricity and magnetism too were
                     initially conceived as particular imponderable matters. With
                     electricity, as is well known, one soon arrived at the idea
                     of imagining two opposed matters, two "fluids," a positive
                     and a negative, which in the normal state mutually
                     neutralized each other until they were separated from one
                     another by a so-called "electric force of separation." One
                     could then charge two bodies, one with positive and the
                     other with negative electricity; when the two were connected
                     through a third, conducting body, the equalization would
                     then take place, depending on circumstances, either suddenly
                     or by means of a continuous current. The sudden equalization
                     appeared very simple and illuminating, but the current posed
                     difficulties. Against the simplest hypothesis — that in the
                     current either only positive or only negative electricity
                     moves each time — Fechner, and in more elaborate development
                     Weber, opposed the view that in the closed circuit two equal
                     currents of positive and negative electricity always flow
                     simultaneously in opposite directions, in channels lying
                     between the ponderable molecules of the bodies.
                     (Engels 1925)

It is easy to counterpose to this a theory with a unified agent — in Reich's case
the orgone — but then one cannot, as Reich does in his fictional conversation with
an "electrophysicist," sweep the equally simple theory of electrons and charge
separation under the table in order to win an argument against a straw man.

www.orgonomie.net
                                        48

Does this mean, therefore, that Reich's experiments on "contact electricity,"
which he conducted between 1939 and roughly 1944 and with whose help he explained,
among other things, the functioning of the orgone energy accumulator, are
hopelessly dilettantish? Let us leave the theory aside and look at a concrete
example: when ordinary adhesive tape is rapidly unrolled, electromagnetic
radiation is emitted. In a vacuum, X-rays can even be produced! In everyday life
we encounter this so-called "triboluminescence" when, for instance, we tear open
self-adhesive envelopes in the dark.


---


Although the phenomenon of triboluminescence has been known
                    for several decades, physicists still cannot fully explain
                    the effect. The crux of the matter is above all an immense
                    increase in energy density, which then triggers the glow.
                    Adhesive tape, for example, produces bluish light even when
                    unrolled normally, although the surface bond contains 100
                    times less energy than is needed for a photon of visible
                    light — let alone for the far more energetic X-radiation.
                    (NN 2008)

The physicists at the University of California who describe this phenomenon
naturally try to explain the whole thing with "charge separation" and
accelerating electrons. The student of orgonomy can answer, in the simplest
way imaginable, the question that remains unanswered: where is the enormous
amount of missing energy supposed to come from!

3.b. The Atomic Nucleus
After the development of the orgone energy accumulator, let us make the leap to
the second milestone of orgone physics: In the 1951 report The ORANUR Experiment I,
Reich describes how radioactivity acts upon concentrated orgone energy and
transforms it into a different, so to speak, "radioactive" state ("ORANUR")
(Reich 1951b). In 1957, in The ORANUR Experiment II (Contact with Space), he
showed that this influence is reciprocal, i.e., in the ORANUR experiment the
radioactive substances themselves were also transformed in such a way that
their radioactive decay changed (Reich 1957).

In mechanistic science there is, from the outset, no place for "orgone energy,"
and radioactive decay is considered, in principle, beyond influence. Some time
ago, however, New Scientist showed that this bastion of mechanistic science is
slowly but surely crumbling. In 1986, Dave Alburger and his colleagues at the
Brookhaven National Laboratory on Long Island, New York, evaluated the data of a
four-year continuous measurement of the half-life of the radioactive isotope
silicon-32. The predicted half-life of 172 years did indeed emerge, but the
decay rate was inexplicably highest in February and lowest in August. In 1998,
a team at the Physikalisch-Technische Bundesanstalt in Braunschweig evaluated
the decay rate of radium-226 measured over 15 years and likewise found an annual
deviation. Further research findings concern seasonal fluctuations of half a
percent in the decay of plutonium-238, which the medical physicist Ken Ellis at
Baylor College of Medicine in Houston, Texas, observed over a period of nine
years.


---


Alvin Sanders, a physicist at the University of Tennessee in Knoxville, found that
when one compares the C-14 method of age determination, which is based on the
radioactive decay of the isotope carbon-14, with the counting of tree rings,
fluctuations with a period of 200 years occur. And finally, at the GSI Helmholtz
Centre for Heavy Ion Research in Darmstadt, fluctuations in the decay rate with a
period of 7 seconds were found in the two radioactive heavy ions praseodymium-140
and promethium-142.

Ephraim Fischbach and Jere Jenkins of Purdue University in West Lafayette,
Indiana, are attempting to integrate these research findings into a unified theory:
the three fluctuations (a seasonal one, one of 200 years, and one of 7 seconds)
could possibly be explained with the help of solar neutrinos.

In beta decay, as occurs for example in silicon-32, a neutron in the atomic
nucleus transforms into a proton by emitting an electron and an antineutrino.
Fischbach and Jenkins now speculate that a neutrino striking a neutron would
likewise produce a proton and an electron, so that the "autonomy" of radioactive
decay would be suspended. This, in turn, would explain the seasonal fluctuation
by the fact that over the course of the year the Earth moves away from the
neutron source, the Sun, and back toward it again.

www.orgonomie.net
                                          50

Correspondingly, the above-mentioned period of 200 years can be explained by the
corresponding fluctuation in solar activity. And as for the 7 seconds, that could
be attributed quantum-mechanically to "neutrino oscillation" (Mullins 2009).

Be that as it may: with every passing year, Reich's "absurd" ORANUR experiment
looks less absurd!

That people are still engaging with Reich's physics 75 years after the ORANUR
experiment is due, first, to the fact that the orgone actually exists21 and,
second, because Reich can be "translated." For example, one must — entirely in
the spirit of Ernst Mach — simply forget that matter is composed of molecules
and atoms, and proceed only from what one actually sees. In such a world there
can be no charge separation, and matter is destroyed by mere boiling. Then one
must engage with Reich within the framework of this world and see where he
leads. After all, without his half-educated physics he would never have
discovered the orgone energy accumulator and the cloudbuster.

In "translating" the physical reactionary Reich into a language compatible with
today's physics, however, one falls into the danger into which most have fallen
so far. On the one hand, one "erases" all genuinely orgonotic phenomena (because
one does not engage with Reich's "erroneous" premises), and on the other hand,
one explains any remainder with "action structures," "additional dimensions," and
other esoteric set pieces which, compared with the orgone, stand even further
outside the scientific mainstream and will be completely forgotten by tomorrow.


---


I'm sorry, but I'd rather remain an "orgonomic backwoodsman"! The only thing that
can be at issue is seeking orgonotic functions within the prevailing physical
theories.

21
  No matter how "hopelessly outdated" and downright erroneous Reich's conceptions
of certain physical concepts may also have been.

www.orgonomie.net
                                        51

Conversely, there is simply no place in physics for the orgone: mechanics,
electromagnetism, quantum mechanics, gravitation, the two nuclear forces —
that's it. Of course, the whole thing can be expanded to explain phenomena that do
not belong to the everyday and are not arbitrarily repeatable, such as
"parapsychological phenomena." Burkhardt Heim attempted this with an extremely
complicated approach. But that merely spreads the territory of mechanistic
physics still further — leaving even less room for the orgone.

Some consider everything that has happened since 1900, and especially since 1905,
to be a wrong turn. We should, they say, return to the ether physics of the 19th
century. To this it must be objected that this physics is the epitome of everything
mechanistic. And contrary to the superficial impression, Reich was by no means a
friend of this approach. He repeatedly pointed out that it seemed far too
mechanistic to him himself when he used concepts like "flowing," "accumulating,"
"density," etc. This language, borrowed from the mechanics of gases and liquids,
misses the character of orgone energy (Reich 1949a, p. 156).

The matter recalls the position of psychoanalysis at the beginning of the last
century, when it was reproached for unsustainable theory-building by the
developing psychology on the one side and by the philosophy of science on the
other. This was, among other things, Alfred Adler's motive for breaking with
Freud. In the early 1920s, Reich defended the scientific character of
psychoanalysis against these skeptics. If one applies the same method lege artis
(psychoanalysis), one arrives again and again at the same results. And precisely
that is the crux of the whole matter: one can test scientific hypotheses only
within a given framework, so that one can always be accused of circular reasoning.
For example, the skeptics of that era accused Einstein of pseudoscience, since
his claim that the speed of light is the same relative to every frame of reference
rested on a pure circular argument — for after all, it was measured with light
signals.
