"Negative-Mass Hydrodynamics in a Spin-Orbit-Coupled Bose-Einstein Condensate" (Ghose 2017). The point is this: when one exerts a force on this matter, it does not move away from one, accelerating according to "F = m × a" ("expanding"), but quite the contrary, comes toward one ("contracting").

To this end, rubidium atoms were squeezed together in a tiny horizontal tube and cooled to near absolute zero. In this state, the movements of the atoms are so correlated that they move like a single unified wave. According to the common understanding, one can use laser light to push the atoms out of the opened tube, but instead of expanding in that direction, the "Bose-Einstein condensate" contracts in the opposite direction, "comes toward one." With the contrast between positive and negative (inertial!) mass, one involuntarily thinks of that between mechanical potential and orgonomic potential.


---


We were at bosons. Another group of subatomic and atomic particles, the "fermions," is grasped by means of "Fermi-Dirac statistics." The building blocks of matter belong to them—above all, electrons, protons, and neutrons. According to the "exclusion principle" formulated by Pauli in 1924, two fermions can never exist in the same state. The otherwise identical electrons, for example, differ by two possible orientations of the "spin." Fermions are, so to speak, entangled with one another by their "handedness."46 Because, due to this mechanism, the lowest energy level is occupied very quickly, then the second, the third, etc., structure can arise in this way. Bosons would gather together in the lowest energy level and form a structureless "energy sea." With the help of this difference between fermions and bosons, one can locate in modern physics the distinction between matter (mass) and energy, so significant for orgonomy. Since Einstein's Special Theory of Relativity (1905), after all, it has been fashionable to act as though this division were obsolete (cf. sections 4.b. and 4.c.).

The "fermionic" structure of matter becomes evident in the spectral lines discovered by Fraunhofer in 1814, which become visible in (emitting or absorbing) gases under low pressure—that is, when the atoms, unlike in solids, do not disturb one another, and so the characteristic structure of the individual atom can come into its own. Planck was to discover the "bosonic" quantum structure of energy on the basis of the continuous spectrum of solids. Bohr accomplished the corresponding feat for matter on the basis of the spectral lines.

6.a. The functional realm "coexistent effect"
Let us first consider the easily grasped functional realm "relative motion," which we can throughout capture by means of "distance covered against elapsed time span," length (L) against time (t), L/t:

We measure time with clocks—that is, with oscillations, such as those of a pendulum. If these oscillations propagate through space, we have waves before us. We can equate waves with the spinning wave, and oscillations with pulsation (back-and-forth oscillation) (see Reich 1957, p. 131 on this).

If we proceed from ourselves and our body, relative motion in general and the spinning wave in particular dominate, at least during the day. In movement, especially in walking, the pendulum becomes the wave! Consider any animal whatsoever as it moves about: it traces a version of the spinning wave into space. In sleep, on the other hand, when our body is largely paralyzed, we go over entirely into the autonomic nervous system, which is pulsation become structure: contraction = sympathetic, expansion = parasympathetic.

The expression "a light goes on for him," when one begins to understand something, expresses this well. Concepts like "enlightenment" or "insight" point in the same direction.

The content of the dream, and of consciousness in general, is described by the function of attraction, which splits into the two variations "associating" (connecting) and "dissociating" (separating). One may think, for instance, of surrealist films, where on the one hand the most separate things are strung together in such a way that their sequence yields "associatively" more or less "meaningful" connections (black hair – black asphalt – abyssally "black" thoughts – free fall), or the viewer, through all the confusion with its unpredictable "jumps," is himself put into a dissociative state and experiences derealization. Religion and so-called "spirituality" (one need only think of near-death experiences) really deal with nothing else—that is, with associating and dissociating.

While in the functional realm of "relative motion" precisely that—namely motion—determines everything, that is, everything follows on everything else without break, in the functional realm of "coexistent effect" jumps occur. Correspondingly, in classical physics structures are arbitrarily divisible. A planet, for example, can circle the sun "through the ether" at any distance whatsoever, just as a ship can in principle take any route across an ocean. It is different in the microcosm, where the coexistent effect predominates: an electron moving around an atomic nucleus can do so only at certain distances. Between the electron orbits the electron is not defined; it is simply nonexistent there,

6.b. The error in "Ea and the Wave Function"
There are thus two realms of orgonotic functioning: motion (spinning wave and pulsation) and coexistent effect (radiation and attraction). The first realm determines our everyday life; the second determines, for example, our nocturnal dreams. In Ea and the Wave Function
(www.orgonomie.net/hdodorea.htm) I attempted to understand the UFO phenomenon ("Ea") within the framework of the first realm, but the second appears to be far more important here. I had wanted to explain Ea entirely within the framework of spinning wave and pulsation—that is, with the transition from the "blurred" wave/expansion to the "concentrated" pulse/contraction of the spinning wave, and vice versa.

The orgone energy accumulator, the cloudbuster, and the medical DOR-buster, and not least gravitation, have throughout primarily to do with radiation and attraction. Radiation passes over into attraction, and vice versa. The common functioning principle of these two factors is the coexistent effect: simultaneity and "co-locality" in the sense of Newton's "action at a distance" in gravitation, or of "entanglement" in quantum mechanics.


---


Following Einstein, who had made time a "fourth dimension," models with "additional dimensions" were introduced again and again. One can naturally do that with anything whatsoever. For example, one could plot the ripeness of an apple from "green" to "red" on an axis of a coordinate system and in this way create a "dimension." The concept "dimension" obviously invites abuse! Category error! Dimensions define the freedom of motion: in a narrow, so-to-speak "one-dimensional" tube, a grain of dust can move only back and forth; in a cavity defining a two-dimensional surface, additionally sideways; and in free three-dimensional space, finally, additionally up and down. Time becomes a further degree of freedom, because it defines durations and the sequence of "points in time."49

One can make the difference between motion and coexistent motion concrete using the coordinate system with whose help motion is measured. We have, on the one hand, the coordinate system and, on the other hand, the graph that one can draw in it. The graph stands for motion, while the coordinate system itself is an example of coexistent effect, for if, for example, the y-axis represents space (which we here reduce to one length dimension, L), then this axis is itself, so to speak, "simultaneous"—that is, free of motion. Spatial distances are measured with the help of this "yardstick," which makes sense only if the yardstick itself is, "in itself," completely rigid. The coordinate system itself is motionless, which also holds for the x-axis, which symbolizes time (t). On it I can measure time intervals, but to apply motion, and thereby the dimension of time, to the axis itself would be absurd.50 L and t in the functional realm "motion" are simple variations:

6.c. Schrödinger's Cat Comes to Visit
There are constructs—for example, "Santa Claus" or "urban legends," like the gigantic "terror birds" in the USA—about which the agreement exists that they do not exist, but which nevertheless partly take on an "existence" similar to that of "Schrödinger's cat," to which I will come back shortly. In fact—consult folklorists, ethnologists, parapsychologists, researchers of religion, "cryptozoologists," etc.—there are sufficient indications that such chimeras can become something like "reality." One can see this alone from the mutually exclusive religions: all of them have their "apparitions." It is entirely possible for a child to come up with evidence for the existence of Santa Claus—for example, inexplicable footprints on the roof. The world is full of the craziest stories. Only a personality-disordered ignoramus or a pseudoscientific "skeptic" can close his eyes to this. These phenomena always remain in suspension—that is, one will never catch the "Santa Claus" or "Bigfoot" or any other phenomenon of folklore red-handed, but neither can one simply deny them any reality whatsoever. They resemble "Schrödinger's cat," which remains for an hour in a mystical suspended state.

Take any atomic decay process—for example, a decaying atomic nucleus or an excited electron shell. Let us say there is a 50 percent probability that within the next hour a subatomic particle will be emitted. This fact, established experimentally on identical processes, is exhaustively described by Schrödinger's quantum wave function. Now let us imagine that the released electron or photon is caught by a detector, which in turn triggers a mechanism that kills Schrödinger's cat. The whole thing takes place in a closed box, which is opened after an hour. Remarkably, as long as the box remains closed, the state of the cat is likewise completely described by the said wave function. Correspondingly, the cat hovers for an hour in a "smeared" state between life and death. Only when the box is opened does the wave function "collapse" into a definite state: "dead cat" or "living cat."


---


The question is when exactly wave functions collapse. In our case, would it also collapse if no human being, but a second detector in the box, registers the death of the cat? Why then did it not collapse with the first detector? What if this "second detector" is a human being who is stuck inside the box? It is obvious that it is human consciousness that brings the wave function to collapse—or, more precisely, collective consciousness: the unanimous, i.e., "objective," determination that the cat is dead or alive. If one now considers that absolutely everything in this universe can be traced back to quantum phenomena, one could draw the conclusion that reality is nothing other than a "collective agreement."

What is consciousness? First of all, consciousness has no space—that is, it has no parts. It is similarly "smeared" as "Schrödinger's cat" (the

www.orgonomie.net
                                          123

quantum wave function), which for an hour is, so to speak, "only time" and has no spatial parts. Orgonometrically this is expressed as follows:

One can exploit this to cover distances that, viewed "spatially," cannot be managed. First one needs a point of connection—for example, telepathy—in order afterwards to build out this "channel." For that one must pay a price. First, one will be able to reach only worlds that largely resemble one's own, and second, one is condemned to an existence resembling that of "Santa Claus." Telepathy is possible only with beings who are largely identical with oneself, so that something like "resonance" (radiation) can occur. For this reason we encounter exclusively "humanoids" who are, wondrously, adapted to our ecosystem. At the same time they are so chimera-like: they resemble ghosts more than those "extraterrestrial visitors" as we imagine them from "Star Trek."

They have a spatial existence on another planet at a distance that in principle cannot be overcome (or let us rather say: not economically). Therefore they put themselves into a state corresponding to that of Schrödinger's cat, in which spatial distances play no role. Their manifestation on this planet accordingly depends on our collective consciousness—a consciousness that, guided by telepathy, has led them here.

Their goal is the impossible—that is, to manifest themselves here on Earth permanently. If the invasion becomes too obvious, the wave function collapses immediately and the "channel" is closed. If they do not manifest themselves at all, everything remains in suspension and the "channel" is no channel at all. Their only possibility is a behavior such as we actually observe: a slow seeping in, literally from the blurred edge of our perception.

I know no other explanation for UFOs and extraterrestrials that fits even approximately as precisely. It explains not only why the extraterrestrials are so similar to us and why they are so ghostly, but also, for example, why this invasion drags on over aeons. It explains the practically

6.d. John von Neumann and Terrestrial-Extraterrestrial
Hybrids
Hybrid animals are created when germ cells of different species find one another to form an embryo. A mule, for example, is the offspring of a female horse and a male donkey. Chimeras, named after creatures from Greek mythology, are artificially created by combining genetic material from different species.

The first successful human-animal chimeras were reported in the year 2003. Chinese researchers at Shanghai Second Medical University successfully fused human cells with rabbit eggs. The scientists were permitted to allow the eggs to develop for several days in a petri dish. Their hope was that this process could one day be used to grow cells or tissue for transplants.

One could also mention that Europeans are obviously hybrid beings: crossbreeds between Homo sapiens and another animal species, Homo neanderthalensis.

To make my assessment of the problem of terrestrial-extraterrestrial hybrids comprehensible, I must go out on a limb: I believe the matter of the extraterrestrials and their relationship to Homo sapiens is understandable less from a perspective à la "Star Trek" and more from one à la "Poltergeist." Take the "Urzi case" from Italy.53 It is all objective, before the camera, with all kinds of witnesses, similar parallel cases, etc. But on the other hand the case is completely absurd: the heavenly phenomena are clearly a show for a single person, Herr Urzi. Other people like me never observe UFOs, but this fellow simply looks out of his window at random and—there they are. Also, the flying objects he films are completely absurd: discs, Adamski UFOs, orbs, and even seemingly living "space snakes." From a "Star Trek perspective" this makes no sense whatsoever.

Still worse are encounters with extraterrestrials and abduction cases: "spacemen" of all kinds and "races," even "Nordic extraterrestrials," including "Nordics" with very large almond-shaped black eyes.

7. Truth and Reality
7.a. The superficial level
Commonly—that is, in the liberal worldview of the West and in the "wisdom of the East"—truth and reality (and with them, ought and is) are strictly separated, since, after all, there can be different viewpoints (angles from which one regards the one reality) and therefore different truths.

With the distinction between truth and reality, however, one falls for a "bourgeois ideology," whereas for the dialectical materialist a truth always proves itself in practice. For Nietzsche, truth is a function of justice, which in turn always entails a judging (that is, an acting).

If one were right with the distinction between truth and reality, there would also be a difference between path and goal, but for Reich the path is already the goal. And one would also imply that spirit ("the standpoint") existed independently of nature; according to Reich, however, all such assumptions are mystical and therefore completely remote from reality.

At the same time, the equals sign between truth and reality must be replaced by the functional equality symbol, which points us to the deeper common functional level that influences both elements, which then no longer lead a separated, "ideal" existence. This functional level, the common functioning principle (CFP), is contact, which, however, is in turn closely correlated with the function of the ego. Without ego, as in extreme psychosis, there is no contact, no truth, and no reality.56


---


In place of reality, unreality and the counter-truth of the neurotic step forward—he who sees everything in the light of his irrational fears, aggressions, lusts, etc. The pestilential character ("Modju") exploits this structural helplessness (infantilism) to push through his counter-reality and the untruth. One may think, for example, of the persecution of "cosmopolitan" dissidents in state socialism.

The real problem accordingly lies not in the realm of truth and reality, but naturally in contact—that is, ultimately in the biophysical structure of the human being. For the armored human being, truth becomes a deadly danger, for in his contactlessness he can only wreak havoc with the truth, and the truth can wreak havoc on him—which is why, according to Reich, the counter-truth, which suits the armored human being and fits his unreality, must be protected from the truth. To constantly hurl truths about merely for the satisfaction of ego needs is simply criminal. Moreover, it is a typical Modju tactic: by aiming truths at others, he keeps the truth away from himself, so that the truth ultimately becomes a lie.

7.c. Cosmic superposition and ORANUR
If one looks at physics as it is taught in high schools and universities, one gets the impression that orgone energy is completely superfluous. But nothing could be further from reality, as one can show with the help of Poincaré, Planck, Bohr, Heisenberg, and Feynman.

After Newton, Maxwell, and Boltzmann had done their work, the description of the universe seemed complete. Everything was explained in terms of mechanics and electromagnetism. Poincaré was the first to destroy the promise of mechanics, by introducing what is known today as "chaos theory." Quite simply, he tried to describe the solar system in all its details, and soon recognized that it cannot be stable when we consider all the planets, moons, and the sun, and their mutual gravitational interactions. As with the famous "butterfly effect" in climatology, all these tiny gravitational effects would soon run up against gigantic effects that would tear the solar system apart. The precise "cosmic clockwork" handed down by Ptolemy, Copernicus, Kepler, and Newton is an illusion! As Reich emphasized, only the contraction of cosmic orgone energy through the streaming-in cosmic superposition stabilizes the solar system and every other planetary and galactic system in the universe (Reich 1951a).59


---


The same can be said of the microcosm, which was opened up by statistical mechanics (thermodynamics) and the elucidation of electromagnetism. This ordered world, too, collapsed when Planck was confronted with the "ultraviolet catastrophe." If electromagnetic radiation can pass over into ever smaller wavelengths, the universe would simply explode like a hydrogen bomb! And in general: if everything can become smaller and smaller without end, if an infinity opens up here in the small, the energy content of every smaller part of space would be infinite! For this reason Planck's quantum of action h was introduced. Similar to the solar system, it made possible—as Bohr was the first to formulate—the stability of atoms, for without Planck's quantum of action, every electron circling the atomic nucleus would crash into the nucleus, due to its acceleration, through the emission of ever shorter wavelengths of electromagnetic radiation.

But even Planck and Bohr could not save the universe from explosion, for Heisenberg stepped up and did the inevitable: Planck's quantum of action implies the uncertainty relation. If in the microcosm we reduce the distances (length) ever further, the momentum can take on any value; and if I choose ever smaller observation time windows, the energy can exceed any arbitrary scale. It is just another version of the "ultraviolet catastrophe"—that is, the universe should once again explode like a hydrogen bomb! In Feynman's quantum electrodynamics, which explains everything except gravity and radioactivity (that is, cosmic superposition and

Why is reality, and with it truth, so contradictory? Against my remarks in part 7.c. one can object that cancer and other biopathies are sympathetic, not parasympathetic. Well, cancer is of course sympathetic, but what about the formation of protozoan cancer cells through the reorganization of blue bions from the original cancer tissue? What about the emergence and spread of tumors, an unambiguously parasympathetic process? (see, for example, Zhang 2016 on this). After all, "cancer" is nothing other than the endeavor of the organism to reorganize itself after all the sympathetic decay (albeit on a more primitive level). Also, in its initial stages cancer tissue can scarcely be distinguished from inflamed tissue. The carcinomatous cancer biopathy is a sympathetic theater with many parasympathetic events in its halls, similar to parasympathetic asthma, which has a sympathetic core. One may also think of all the parasympathetic inflammatory diseases of the bowel, the skin, etc.

The same is happening today in American and European society with the formation of "cancer cells"—that is, communist cells—the formation of "tumors" in Seattle, Stuttgart, Berlin, Hamburg, and elsewhere, the left-radical in-flammation everywhere, etc.

This resembles character analysis in the psychological realm, with all the drives and counter-drives (defenses) working against one another while being organized as a whole like an onion. In somatic biopathies we have similar layers of sympathetic and parasympathetic innervations. The same holds for social biopathies—for example, the extremely authoritarian Nazi regime, which beneath the surface bore clearly antiauthoritarian traits, just as the Adenauer democracy of the postwar period, beneath its "democratic-free" exterior, had many extremely reactionary, restorative traits.


---


In all realms, human reality is in this way contradictory within itself: as with an onion, beneath every impulse one finds a counter-impulse, beneath which in turn a counter-impulse to that one, and so on ever further down to the core. One must know this in order to make even approximate progress in psychotherapy, in sociology ("politics"), and in medicine. Otherwise one will inevitably fail wretchedly at the "contradictory reality," as all approaches so far have ultimately failed wretchedly, without exception. Outside orgonomy—that is, in the armored world—there is, in the end, only the lie, or "truths" that do not take the counter-truths into account and are therefore worse than lies!

APPENDIX

John Wilder: The Bauhaus and Goethe
             60

(2013)
Although there are numerous theories of color that have been presented to the public over the centuries, two theories stand out: that of Isaac Newton, an Englishman, and that of Johann Wolfgang von Goethe, a German, for both have been influential for centuries right up to our own century. At the Bauhaus these two theories competed with one another and influenced each other, but since the Bauhaus had been founded in Goethe's hometown of Weimar, and the first directors (Itten, Kandinsky, Klee) were expressionist artists who applied and taught color theories ultimately based on Goethe's work, Goethe's view prevailed.

Goethe, 1749-1832, moody and artistic, experimented in an exploratory way, mixing pigments without much theoretical guidance, and recorded revealing visual and emotional reactions. Newton, 1642-1727, a strict Puritan, was first and foremost a theorist, and he proceeded from the assumption that his light experiments would control his theories about the properties of light rays projected through a prism.

Goethe's more subjective theory, grounded in work with pigments, was of particular interest to practicing artists, while physicists preferred Newton's objective theory (which managed without emotional involvement). To a certain degree the differences between Newton and Goethe are reflected in their respective languages: in English there is one word for "mind" and another word for "soul," whereas in German a single word means both.61

I believe that an earlier scientific disagreement can throw some light on the Newton/Goethe controversy. The different approaches to the study of color held by Newton and Goethe were preceded by different approaches to the study of motion held by Newton and another German, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz. Newton's famous studies of motion were motivated by theory, while Leibniz's studies had another, more spiritual side. For example:


---


(Newton) Momentum = mass × velocity
(Leibniz) Vis Viva = mass × velocity × velocity

60
   Reprint of the translation from the American with the kind permission of the
author. First published 2013 in Blog Nachrichtenbrief.
61
   In English there is no word for the comprehensive concept "Geist"! [Translator's
note by the German author.]

www.orgonomie.net
                                          136

"Vis viva" was Leibniz's term for life force or living force, in which he showed interests similar to Goethe's. Newton's more sterile approach developed into "force = mass × acceleration," while Leibniz's approach developed into the formula for kinetic energy, the energy of motion (KE = 1/2 mass × velocity × velocity). Today engineers use the formulas going back to Leibniz to describe motions microscopic and macroscopic, whether Brownian molecular motion or planetary motions. Although physicists use Newton's representation of planetary motions—"force vectors" acting on the planets—Leibniz's representation is more practical for engineers. It requires that the total energy (the changing kinetic and potential energies of the planet) remain constant. Thus the practical approach of the engineers gave preference to Leibniz's approach in the investigation of bodies in motion, just as the practical approach of the visual artists gave preference to Goethe's approach in the investigation of colors that influence emotions. At the Bauhaus, the different approaches were to meet through the careful integration of the artist/craftsman and the technician in the workshop.

In the early Bauhaus, Goethe's views clearly had precedence. Johannes Itten, who created the Bauhaus's introductory course for new students in 1919, used Goethe's discoveries about colors, as mediated to him by his mentors Hölzel, Runge, and the "spiritual scientist" Rudolf Steiner. The theosophist Rudolf Steiner had devoted many years to the publication of Goethe's books and built the Goetheanum in Dornach, Switzerland, where he held at least ten lectures on the theory of colors. Hölzel's theory of the seven color contrasts derived directly from Goethe's studies. In Itten's introductory course, the seven Hölzel/Goethe contrasts were each investigated objectively, emotionally, and symbolically according to hue, value, temperature, complementarity, simultaneity, saturation, and extension. Itten believed, like Goethe, in the ability of art to bring the human being into stronger contact with his soul, or spirit.


---


Kandinsky and Klee likewise held fundamental courses on color at the Bauhaus, and they continued teaching long after Itten left in 1923. Kandinsky, likewise an adherent of Steiner's theosophy, had written: "Thus, behind matter, within matter, the creative spirit is hidden. The veiling of the spirit in matter is often so dense that, in general, there are few people who can see the spirit through it" (Kandinsky 1912). Like Itten and Klee, Kandinsky also wanted to reduce form to its simplest essence, to its minimum, so that the inner spirit or soul of the artist, of the artist group, of the people, or of the age could be expressed more strongly in the outer form. Form could be simplified through the shattering of linear perspective and contours, by making color and form transparent, through the banishment of shadows and highlights, or through the adoption of a regular patterning. It was believed that simplicity of form would emphasize color more strongly and improve the interaction of the colors with one another. The painter Josef Albers, who was trained by Itten, Kandinsky, and Klee, later studied the psychological effect of colors and color combinations and

www.orgonomie.net
                                       137

contrasts, reducing form to a minimum. With Albers it was such that he painted color squares within color squares.

It seems to me that artistic, dynamic color interactions were offered as a royal road to the expression of the soul. Klee wrote that he sought the bridge that leads from the visible to the invisible. "Art does not reproduce the visible, but makes visible" (Klee 1920). Klee wanted to derive his ideas from everyday experiences, quite as scientists and engineers do. Klee, like them, also clothed his ideas in terms of motion, energy, and charge (see his notebooks). It seems to me that Klee, in his approach, is more American—more empirical and democratic—than Kandinsky or Itten, who both apparently tended toward authoritarian mysticism.


---


When, in 1923, the expressionist Itten was replaced by the Russian constructivist and communist Moholy-Nagy, the Bauhaus shifted its emphasis from "art for art's sake" to purposeful art, from individual spirituality to communal socialism, from expressionism to architecture and industrial design. Art (and artistic color interaction) was now regarded as a power that could transform not only individuals but whole societies. It could help in the building of a new world through a unified worldview, created through modern art with the help of modern technology. Instead of a few individuals discovering a more genuine, expressive contact with their spirit, the whole of society could transform itself through "Soviet" (workers' council) architecture and through the artistic design of mass production, through the awakening and the visualization of the slumbering "folk spirit," and see the world as Goethe might have seen it.

www.orgonomie.net

62

John Wilder: Vis Viva (2013)
Wilhelm Reich was a product of a German education, not a British or French one, so at school he was likely permeated by the views of the great German scientist Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, and was likely made familiar at school with Leibniz's concept of the integral "life force" inherent in the material world, instead of the colder, more abstract worldview of a Newton and Descartes, which prevailed in England and, to a lesser degree, in France.

Before modern European scientists began to use the term "energy," the term vis viva (from the Latin for "life force") was in use.


---


Leibniz was convinced of the significance of the product of "mass and velocity squared," which had originally been investigated by Huygens and which Leibniz called vis viva, living force. He believed that vis viva was the true measure of force, in contrast to Descartes's force of motion (which corresponds to "mass times velocity," i.e., momentum). It is not entirely clear why Leibniz chose "m v²" as the quantity instead of Descartes's "m v," but he apparently came, through mechanical considerations, to the conclusion that his quantity was more fundamental. Leibniz's claim that vis viva, not Descartes's quantity, was the most fundamental conserved quantity comes very close to an early formulation of the law of conservation of energy of mechanics. Since, however, the conserved quantity of motion had become one of the pillars of Cartesian natural philosophy, Leibniz's proposal—that the fundamental quantity of motion was a different one from that which Descartes had proposed—was rejected from the outset by all good Cartesians. A great controversy followed between the German school of physical thought, which naturally supported Leibniz, and the schools of France and England, whose Cartesians and Newtonians turned against him. In determining vis viva as the fundamental quantity of motion, Leibniz sought a principle of action that remained conserved and that kept the universe from "running down."

From 1686 onward, Leibniz wrote a series of works in which he objected that the quantity that remains absolutely and indestructibly conserved in nature is not the quantity of motion "m v" (mass times the absolute measure of velocity), but rather the vis viva, or living force, "m v²" (mass times velocity times velocity). The living force, measured with "m v²," formed, for Leibniz, the essence of nature, an all-encompassing principle, the foundation of his whole philosophy. Thus the early controversy over vis viva is no meaningless quarrel over "momentum" versus "kinetic energy," but a skillful objection by Leibniz against an inadequate concept, "m v," and how it describes the world.

Motion and extension, for Descartes the essence of nature, were for Leibniz merely relations and did not constitute reality. What was

62
  Reprint of the translation from the American with the kind permission of the
author. First published 2013 in Blog Nachrichtenbrief.

www.orgonomie.net
                                          139

truly real in nature, for Leibniz, was the primal force, or striving, and this was elaborated by him in the following years as the essence of the monad, so named by him.

What is real in the universe, for Leibniz, is activity; the essence of substance is action, not extension, as Descartes had insisted. This activity presents itself as a primal force, or a striving toward change; it is the innermost nature of the body. The indivisible basic substances, whose essence is a continuous tendency toward action, were later designated by Leibniz, in the year 1714, as monads.


---


Thus the conservation of substance and force form the foundation of Leibniz's philosophical standpoint. Since time and space are neither realities nor substances, but merely represent relations, motion—which is the continuous change in space and time—is likewise only a relation. What is real in motion is the force, a momentary state that carries within itself the striving toward a future state. It is therefore clear why motion and extension cannot be, for Leibniz, the essence of reality, as they were for Descartes.

Over the years, the views of Descartes and Newton have prevailed in theoretical physics, but Leibniz's conceptions continue to work, to a certain degree, in applied engineering, in practical work. For example, in celestial dynamics the vis viva equation (also called the orbital energy conservation equation) remains one of the fundamental and useful equations determining the motion of bodies circling the Earth. In modern terminology it is the direct consequence of the theorem of the conservation of total energy, in which the sum of kinetic and potential energy remains constant as the satellite moves in its orbit.

A search on the net for "vis viva" helps one understand the evolution (or regression?) of vis viva, the "life force," into the term "kinetic energy," and other abstract terms, mostly through the pupils of Newton:

[Émilie du Châtelet's] book Institutions de Physique
                      ("Lessons in Physics") appeared in the year 1740; it was
                      conceived as an overview of the new ideas in science and
                      philosophy for her 13-year-old son, but contained complex
                      ideas of the leading thinkers of the time and attempted
                      to reconcile them with one another.
                      In the book she combines the theories of Gottfried Wilhelm
                      Leibniz and the practical observations of Willem 's
                      Gravesande to show that the energy of a moving object is
                      not proportional to its velocity, as had been assumed by
                      Newton, Voltaire, and others, but to the square of its
                      velocity. (In classical physics the correct formula is
                      Ek = 1⁄2 m v², where Ek is the kinetic energy of an
                      object, m the mass, and v the velocity.)63

David Boadella: Some orgonotic
radiation effects. A preliminary  64


---


communication (1958)
This spring I decided to build an orgone energy field meter. I had carried this thought within me for a considerable time, but had found various reasons to put the matter off. There was an emotional resistance in me against this whole idea of light bulbs that glow without being plugged into a circuit. Years ago I had read Reich's description of the field meter in The Cancer Biopathy (Reich 1948), but until recently it had never occurred to me to repeat the field-meter effects described by Reich. Only now did I become aware that in my case a feeling of impotence was connected with the visual demonstration of orgone energy. I had the notion that the strong and unmistakable visual effects were to be expected only at a strong orgone concentration, such as was achieved, for example, in Reich's orgonon at Rangeley. At most, years ago I had put an electric light bulb into an orgone accumulator, left it there to "soak itself up," and waited "for what happens." When nothing happened, my feeling of "not being able to do it" was reinforced. Secretly I doubted the existence of an energy in the atmosphere, and was afraid to attempt to prove its existence. I could, after all, have failed, and "proven" that it does not exist. All these personal factors are interwoven and perhaps make understandable why the present observations, in my particular case, were not made some years earlier.

When I took up the idea of the orgone field meter this spring, I had the distinct feeling of being playful. I wanted to become carefully familiar with the required materials and gradually get a feel for them. I knew that I needed an induction apparatus of some kind and a "luminous tube." Reich writes of the use of a "fluorescent tube." While I was inquiring where I could obtain an induction device cheaply, I bought a 4/- Osram neon indicator bulb (230–250 V), took it home rather skeptically, and expected that it would be (if at all) of little use until I had my induction machine.65

---


Observations with a neon bulb
I stroked the neon bulb gently in a darkened room, and after a minute of handling it, there was a series of red flashes. The flashes varied with the material with which the bulb was rubbed, and with the kind of contact produced between the bulb and the material. The kind of light ranged from a slight flickering on just one side of the bulb to a complete illumination of the bulb, strong enough to give the impression of a weak flashlight.

When it was plugged into a circuit at 240 volts, the glow was brighter (visible in daylight) and continuous, but occurred only at the electrodes. The body of the bulb did not light up.

The neon gas seems very sensitive to rubbing of any kind, but on the body, gentle stroking gave better results than vigorous rubbing. When the bulb was completely encompassed with the fingers, the glow was brighter than when it was rubbed only at the side. My hands produced the strongest effects—especially the fingertips, the crook of the thumb, the ball of the hand, and the outer edge of the hand. It was necessary that the hands be fairly dry; otherwise the effects were reduced or could not be produced at all.

When the neon bulb was held in the hand and rubbed against wood, cloth, silk, metal, glass, and other materials, it likewise lit up, but a more vigorous kind of rubbing was required, and the glow was less bright. When an ebonite rod was charged by rubbing with wool, the neon bulb glowed more weakly when one brought the rod near. Similarly, it glowed when it was brought up to a Plexiglas watch cover after the latter had been rubbed. Whenever one took the bulb away from a surface, it flashed brightly.

This behavior of neon gas may not seem surprising, and we shall deal with the explanation later. These observations are reproduced here, although they may be familiar to many people, simply because they were unknown to me, and because they form an introduction to more stirring and almost wholly unknown, or undocumented, light effects.

www.orgonomie.net
                                                143

Observations on household light bulbs
Encouraged by the unexpected reaction of the neon bulb, I amused myself in the same way with an ordinary household electric light bulb: holding it at the metal end, I rubbed it in various ways. To my astonishment, it too lit up after an initial "warm-up period" of less than a minute. The following observations were made over the course of the evening, and subsequently at intervals during the week:

1. The lighting-up had three principal forms:


---


(a) A general glowing of the whole bulb with a bluish-gray color, so that the entire volume of the bulb was visible in complete darkness. This glowing lasted only briefly, but if the rubbing of the bulb was maintained without interruption, the glow could be kept fairly even.

(b) Bluish-green to white streaks, like flashes, from one side of the bulb to the other. These seemed to indicate a stronger excitation of the bulb.66

(c) Brilliant bluish-green to white slowly moving points of light, or "spots" of points, at many places on the bulb, superimposed on the general glowing. To produce these, a firmer and more sustained rubbing of the bulb seemed necessary. A spiral, twisting movement of the hand around the bulb (while the cap of the bulb was held firmly in the other hand) usually sufficed to produce these points of light.

2. The hands and the bulb had to be completely dry. Drying the bulb to remove condensation was simple, but drying the hands did not seem so simple, since the whole question of individual variation was involved. The first observations of the bulb were made at the end of a week during which I had spent at least half an hour in the orgone accumulator on most evenings, so that it was very likely that this contributed to "drying out" my hands. Some people were not able to make the bulb glow, and it was reported that powdering the hands facilitates the lighting-up. The moisture due to normal sweating is presumably absorbed. Other possible causes of difficulty in making the bulb light up are discussed later in this article.

3. Constant handling of the bulb over a period of, say, an hour or more increased its sensitivity. After the bulb had "become accustomed" to glowing frequently over a certain period, it was made to glow by very light contact with the hands in a way that had not been possible before. At some point it began to approach the sensitivity of the neon bulb, so that it glowed surprisingly when I did not expect it, or when I touched it accidentally. When it had reached this degree of sensitivity,

66
     How this was achieved is discussed below.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         144

it lit up, like the neon bulb, also when rubbed against various parts of the body—e.g., crook of the arm, groin, armpit, back of the arm, etc.—again provided that the body was thoroughly dry. As with the neon bulb, the strongest flashes seemed to occur when the bulb was taken away from the skin.


---


4. When my hand was held for a few moments near the wall of the accumulator, and the bulb was then stroked in the normal way, it seemed that the lighting-up was more intense. It was very difficult to exclude subjective factors, or the possibility that this time I rubbed it extra hard. The only possible control was to rub it immediately with the hand that had not been held near the accumulator wall. In addition, I tried to keep the pressure I applied constant for both hands. I observed a similar increase in intensity after I had run my hand through my hair, and when I stroked the bulb normally after it had lain for a few minutes on the skin of my abdomen.

5. When the bulb was rubbed on metal, wood, glass, and other rigid materials, no lighting-up was noticed. Rubbing on fabric—e.g., wool, cotton, silk—gave no lighting-up at all, unless the material was held in the hand that surrounded the bulb. It was then difficult to exclude the effect of the hand, for the thinner the layer of material between the bulb and the hand, the more the effects resemble those achieved with the bare hand, which so far are the strongest. A silk scarf in the hand seemed to hinder the effect of the hand. For example, when the bulb was excited by the hand, pressed lightly into the palm and then removed without rubbing, there was a slight glow. If this action is repeated several times, the glow repeats too, gradually weakening and becoming hard to reproduce, until the bulb is stroked again. Now, if, after such treatment of the bulb in the palm, the palm is covered with a single layer of silk and the bulb is pressed in again, the glimmer is noticeably weaker and indeed scarcely perceptible.

6. The bulb had been excited in this way for several evenings, and, plugged into a circuit, still lit normally. The tungsten filament finally broke, so that the bulb became useless when plugged in and gave no light at all anymore; but the lighting-up effects described above remained completely unaffected by this. The hands could make the bulb light up, but an electric voltage of 240 volts applied to the terminals could not.

7. When a charged ebonite rod, which had been able to make the neon bulb light up at some distance, was brought near the household bulb, there was no lighting-up at all; nor was there any lighting-up when the bulb was rubbed with the charged rod.

All the above observations were made with two 25-watt bulbs (Spacelite and Vesta). I wanted to find out whether these bulbs are filled with gas or vacuum. I knew that most bulbs are gas-filled, and that completely pumping out the air has been almost entirely abandoned, since, when a


---


www.orgonomie.net
                                         145

gas filling is used, there is a reduction of heat loss. Spacelite lamps of all wattages are sold in cartons marked "gas-filled." A few inquiries established, however, that it is customary for 25-watt and 15-watt bulbs to be left evacuated (since there is apparently no energy saving through gas filling at these low wattages).

My two bulbs were accordingly vacuum bulbs, and the lighting-up that I obtained was radiation in a vacuum. Some theoretical implications will be discussed later.

I now decided to test various bulbs, to see what lighting-up effects they have. I worked with 15-watt, 40-watt, 60-watt, and 150-watt bulbs, and with a flashlight bulb. Also with a clear 25-watt bulb and an Osram vacuum radio tube with three electrodes (triode). The 15-watt bulb gave reactions that were not to be distinguished from those of the 25-watt bulbs. The three gas-filled bulbs of higher power all gave a much weaker, much more flickering, and generally paler lighting-up. The color was hard to determine because of the pallor. It seemed to be something like a "white-gray," without the brilliance of the vacuum bulbs. The normal gas filling is 86% nitrogen and 14% argon, and the expected color of nitrogen lighting-up is yellow-brown. So it is possible that the gas-filled bulbs actually lit up yellow-brown, since pale yellow-brown and "white-gray" are sufficiently similar to be descriptions of the same color effect. The cloudiness and unevenness of the lighting-up was striking. It was as if the gas "stood in the way" of the excitation and inhibited it, "fogged" it.

The low-power flashlight bulb, which could be expected, because of its small size, to be very easily excited, gave no lighting-up at all.

The clear 25-watt bulb likewise lit up with sufficient intensity to make the filament just visible, but the light effects seemed less bright and less clear than those of the frosted bulb. It was as if the frosted glass functioned as a kind of screen on which the lighting-up could be observed. With the clear bulb one had the impression that one "looks through" the lighting-up without a background.

The triode reacted similarly to the clear bulb.


---


A further phenomenon was discovered by chance. I had put the original vacuum bulb into one of the two sockets of an adapter plug, and the neon bulb into the other. The plug was a practical holder that gave the hand something to grip. While I stroked over the vacuum bulb, there was a bright flash in the neon bulb. This happened about three times and then vanished, and I could not repeat it. I thought I had accidentally rubbed my fingers against the neon bulb without noticing. Later in the evening, however, the effect reappeared—always when the radiation of the vacuum bulb was sufficiently strong. This had nothing to do with contact across the gap between the bulbs (a distance of two to three inches), since the lighting-up one obtained when the two bulbs, which were then not inserted in their sockets, were brought close together, was considerably weaker and less like a

www.orgonomie.net
                                        146

flash. Then I discovered why I had not been able to repeat the effect before: the switch of the adapter was in the wrong position. I could now let the effect simply persist, or make it disappear, at will, by switching the switch on or off.

With the gas-filled bulbs, a tiny blue spark at one of the contact pins on the bulb cap was observed several times—when the bulb was being stroked and a tiny crackling could be heard.

So far I have not been able to activate the vacuum or the gas-filled bulb by exciting the neon bulb—neither by approach, contact, or through the adapter plug. Nor one vacuum bulb by another.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         147

Explanations
There are, by and large, three kinds of explanations that can be offered for any given phenomenon: a mechanistic explanation, a functional explanation, or, as a way out, dismissing it as mysticism. It is a familiar experience that when the mechanical worldview is challenged by an event that orthodox science can no longer ignore, it attempts either to explain it away as "merely" this or that, or to laugh it off as "crazy" and mystical, standing outside the realm of serious science.

We recall that the bions were either "just streptococci," or they were "private fantasy"; that the To-T difference in the accumulator either did not exist, or, if it existed, it was "just convection from the tabletop to the ceiling." It was similar when the rise of the fever thermometer was described: either the thermometers were faulty, or the rise in body temperature was produced "subjectively."


---


Despite this theoretical awareness, I was at first cautious and hesitated to draw the conclusion that the radiation of the bulbs was an orgonotic phenomenon. There was a resistance against connecting the vacuum effects with Reich's "Vacor" experiments. It was as if I had feared that someone could say: "There we have them again, the crazy orgonomists, who always jump too hastily to the conclusion that it's the orgone." Somehow there was an element of mysticism in my attitude toward orgonomy: it was as if Reich could do it, if he wanted to, and that it was then "orgonomic" and real. I have already described how this kind of attitude delayed my observations for years. Even when the bulb lit up in my hands, the same resistance was still at work. In a certain way, I too wanted to believe that it is "merely this or that."

Accordingly, I investigated some literature in four closely related fields: lamp technology, bio-electricity and bio-luminescence, electrostatics, and the ionization of gases. I also made contact with a number of different people whom I thought would be able to explain the lighting-up: G.E.C. (General Electric Co., Nottingham), A.E.I. Lamp and Lightning, Nottingham; the Department of Physics at Nottingham University, and the National Physical Laboratory, London.

In none of the books consulted was it mentioned that light bulbs glow when they are rubbed in a dark room. Only one book that Harvey wrote on Living Light refers to the fact that "the mere rubbing of a neon tube makes the gas glow" (Harvey 1940, p. 105).

It seemed reasonable to assume that the phenomenon was not generally known. This assumption found full confirmation from the authorities who were consulted. Their reactions are a gripping subject in themselves, and are quoted rather extensively in what follows:

www.orgonomie.net
                                         148

(a) The spokesman of the G.E.C. said that the matter of the described light effects sounded "odd," and said that presumably something was defective with the bulb; I should return it and exchange it for an intact one.


---


(b) The assistant at A.E.I. with whom I spoke reacted as if he were being questioned on a topic beyond his horizon, and fetched the manager. The manager took a "prickly," fairly aggressive stance, and said that he was of course familiar with such an effect, and was aware that 25-watt bulbs glow bluish-green. When asked for details, he had to admit that he had observed this phenomenon only once, and that at that time an electric voltage of 12,000 volts had been applied to the lamp from an electric generator. He was visibly irritated by the statement that one could rub the bulb with the hands and make it glow that way, and remarked: "Most people, of course, don't treat them like Aladdin's lamps and expect the genie to come out of them." He hastened to add that he was sure that no electrical manufacturer was interested in such a phenomenon, and that in any case he had no doubt that most of them were already familiar with the effect.

(c) The PhD scientist at the university was much more open-minded and seemed "interested." He was interested because, as he said, he had recently noticed something similar when he rubbed a neon tube on his sleeve in the dark. That had surprised him, so he had mentioned it to the staff of the University of Durham who work in physics, yet none of them was familiar with it. He had heard from several people who had bought neon tubes that some of the bulbs glowed and others did not. When asked how he explained these "surprising" effects, doubts overcame him; he became vague and said: "You build up an electrostatic field, I suppose." He had never heard of electric light bulbs that lit up in this way, and clearly did not know what to say when he was told that rubbing on silk diminishes the lighting-up. He suggested that the neon tubes would make good "magic wands" at a party.

(d) An electrician at the Nottingham Electricity Board had never heard of the phenomenon, "except at the fairground." He also said rather sneeringly that it sounded like a good party trick.

(e) At third hand I heard that an electrician in Yorkshire had known of the effect (which, in his opinion, most people could achieve) for some years, actually used it at parties, and regularly amused his friends with it.

(f) An experienced PhD physicist, with knowledge of Reich's work, said that the whole thing was, as far as he knew, unknown and unexplained.

(g) The National Physical Laboratories had still not answered a week after an inquiry. [In a postscript Boadella appended the following:]

We received the following letter, signed by L.A. Sayce, the superintendent of the Light Division of the National Physical Laboratory, before going to press:

Dear Sir,


---


the effect you mention in your letter of May 5th
                    seems certainly to be attributable to the electrification
                    produced by the rubbing of the hand. We find that it can
                    most easily be produced in vacuum lamps, such as were
                    formerly manufactured, in which the vacuum is undoubtedly
                    as good as in newer lamps. This suggests that the principal
                    cause is a discharge in the residual gas left in the
                    lamp—much less intense than in a neon tube, because there
                    is much less gas. At this very low pressure it is probable

The excitation of a discharge within the bulb cannot arise

                    [polyester fiber fabric] certainly manages it.

As for the color, the discharge in the residual air
                    (mainly nitrogen) would be bluish, while the luminescence
                    of the glass would be the well-known grass green, and the
                    overall effect would naturally be blue-green.

We know of no literature references on this question, but
                    we are sure that the simple explanation we have given is,
                    broadly speaking, the correct one. We repeat that the degree
                    of vacuum will determine the effect, so that some
                    well-made modern vacuum lamps might show it only with

Comment by David Boadella: The explanation was clearly designed specifically for the inquiry. "It seems," "we feel," "probably," "broadly speaking," point to difficulties in finding a theory that fits the facts. Note the following contradictions: 1. Mr. Sayce says that the glow (discharge) is "much less intense … because there is much less gas." Yet the vacuum lamps were brighter than the gas-filled bulbs. 2. Mr. Sayce suggests that the "well-known grass-green" glow of the glass would be more intense if one reduced the pressure. But his last sentence says the opposite: "some well-made modern vacuum lamps might show it only with difficulty, or not at all."


---


www.orgonomie.net
                                             150

How gentle stroking can produce "very sudden" changes is not said.

From these reactions it becomes clear that the only people who do not ignore the bulb effects are those who have discovered them by chance for themselves; that there is no "official" explanation for them, because there is no "official" recognition of them; and that the first reaction, when the subject is mentioned, is to act as if it were not fit for "serious scientific" consideration, but, like parapsychological research or flying saucers, deserves only jokes and mockery. Either the whole matter is regarded as a joke (as the attitude of the A.E.I. manager suggests); or I was the victim of a hallucination (as one teacher jokingly proposed); or it is "merely one of those things," a good party trick, and something the physicist, like so many things at the fairground, at séances, or at the fortune-teller's table, need not concern himself with.

The second reaction, which occurs only when the phenomenon is demonstrated unambiguously, or when the physicist is prepared to accept your statement in good faith, involves the attempt to do what the textbooks have not done—explain the fact in terms of the existing theory. ("… an electrostatic field … I suppose").

If the physicist can be persuaded to give a serious answer to the question "What is the energy that makes the bulbs light up?", his answer will move within the bounds of electrostatic concepts. It is assumed that the various phenomena of electrostatics can be explained with the same concept with which the action of batteries and dynamos in generating an electric current is explained. Thus the same electrical units can, if necessary, be used to describe both "static" and "current" effects.


---


The only cases of blue-green radiation in a vacuum that I have found occur when a high electrical voltage is produced between the electrodes of a specially constructed vacuum tube.67 The required voltage obviously varies according to the pressure of the residual gas and the distance between the electrodes, but it can normally be expected to lie in the range of 100 to 1000 volts. This was the range that Reich used in his vacuum experiments (see Reich 1949e) with 0.5 micrometers of pressure and with 15 cm distance between the electrodes. In comparison, the volts cited by the A.E.I. manager for a 25-watt bulb seem high, but this may be due to the fact that light bulbs are not equipped with electrodes.68 As for the role the electrodes played when the 12,000 voltage was applied, I do not know; possibly it was "only" the glass and only the tungsten wiring, or possibly electrodes are not even necessary, which seems probable.

67
   In the case of the one example cited by the A.E.I. manager, the specially
constructed tube and the electrodes were dispensed with, but the high voltage was
retained.
68
   Reich reports elsewhere, however, that he used a voltage of 1000 to 2000 volts
to trigger the vacuum radiation. See Reich 1950.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         151

All the cases of vacuum radiation that I have read about were based on an electrical voltage supplied by wire connection from a dynamo, a battery, or an induction coil of some kind. Only when the "peculiar" event is presented to the physicist—that a light bulb glows without any wire connections—does he see himself forced to the assumption that an electrostatic field can set a vacuum radiation in motion. Before that, all this was irrelevant. But as soon as this assumption has been made in desperation (and the attempt to laugh the whole thing off has failed), a little checking shows that it is untenable, so long as we assume that electrostatic "electricity" is the same as Faradaic electricity, the electric current.


---


The electrical voltage needed to trigger the vacuum radiation falls in a range of hundreds of volts to thousands of volts. We must ask whether it is possible for the hands, gently stroking the bulb, to deliver these high voltages? The question appears ridiculous, "peculiar," and so we tend to evade the whole question and its answer. It has been shown that the electrical charge of the skin surface, measured with the help of the oscillograph, lies in the range of millivolts (Reich 1937). The hands form no exception here. We stand before the fact that the hands, whose voltage is negligible, can "by producing an electrostatic field" generate a voltage that is very considerable and can lead to brilliant light effects.

Further considerations show that the electrostatic theory does not suffice to explain the vacuum radiation. The highest charges of "electrostatics" are produced by the most violent forms of friction, as, for example, in the Wimshurst machine.69 But when the vacuum bulb is rubbed vigorously, even on silk, no glow arises—so long as the hand is not firmly holding the silk, and the silk hinders the glow-producing force of the hand.

The observation that stroking light bulbs makes them glow, while vigorous rubbing produces either a much weaker effect or no effect at all, is thus a direct confirmation of Reich's 1944 assumption that "static electricity in the usual sense is not a current at all." In an article on "orgonotic pulsation" (see Reich 1944) he comes to the conclusion: "It is only a question of convention whether we identify the 'electricity' of the ancients with orgone and [retain] the concept of electricity for the orgonotic phenomena. In that case we would have to develop a new concept for what has been known as electromagnetism since Faraday, Ampère, and Volta. Or we must drop the concept of the electricity of the ancients, call the respective phenomena orgonotic, and restrict the concept of electricity to those phenomena that one achieves through the movement of wires in magnetic fields."

69
  Note of the editor in the next issue of Orgonomic Functionalism:
The high charges produced by the Wimshurst machine were said to have been produced

friction to generate charge. The Wimshurst machine accumulates its charge through

www.orgonomie.net
                                         152


---


(Reich 1944, p. 122). In this connection the reaction of an eleven-year-old boy is interesting, to whom the behavior of the bulb in the dark when rubbed was mentioned in passing. In contrast to the skepticism, the confusion, and the defensive laughter of the experts, he gave a direct, emotional reaction: "Oh, what the heck! That's the electricity in your hands, isn't it?" This boy knew hardly anything about science and certainly knew nothing about millivolts. But he must have felt the energy in his hands, and since he had no better designation for this energy, he called it "electricity."

As soon as one takes this step—distinguishing galvanic electricity from the orgonotic phenomena (of which "statics" is only a special case)—some confusing contradictions begin to make sense. In The Cancer Biopathy, Reich described how he became aware of the fact that the quantity of energy detectable at the skin surface bore no relation to the minimal charge measured in millivolts. He points out that, although we can influence sensitive voltmeters by touching them, "the quantities of this reaction, relative to the energy outputs of our organism, are so minimal [that we see no connection]" (Reich 1948, p. 32).

A similar discrepancy exists in the assumption that what is measured with the electroscope, the standard instrument of electrostatics, can be equated with voltage. In fact we have two radically different forms of energy, both measured in one and the same unit. Reich describes this discrepancy as follows: "We charge an electroscope with energy from our hair, so that the electroscope leaf is deflected by 45° or 90°. We can produce the same effect by applying an electric current of high voltage to the electroscope. One unit of orgone charge corresponds to that voltage which is necessary to produce the same deflection of the electroscope leaf. In this way we find that, if we stroke through our hair just once gently, we can draw off quantities of energy corresponding to several hundred volts" (Reich 1944, p. 113).

The implications are relevant and important. Although a certain quantity of orgone energy may correspond to a given quantity of electrical energy, they are in no way equivalent. Although we can draw off energy "corresponding" to hundreds of volts, we cannot, by stroking our hair, cause a voltmeter to register a deflection indicating that our hair is electrically charged with a voltage of hundreds of volts. Effects that can be produced very easily and simply with the orgone energy from the hands can be reproduced only by an electrical voltage a thousand times greater than the electrical voltage of the hands.


---


A useful result of these findings is that they provide an answer to those critics of Reich's interpretations of his Vacor experiments who claim that he did "nothing new," and that his blue radiation in a vacuum under electrical voltage represents a well-established fact in classical physics and has nothing to do with orgone energy. What was new was not the production of bluish radiation in a vacuum, but the recognition that this is functionally identical with other examples of bluish radiation in nature

www.orgonomie.net
                                       153

and can be explained orgonomically just as well, if not better. As far as I know, Reich never demonstrated radiation in the vacuum produced by the hands alone. The fact that this is possible is a convincing confirmation of the orgonomic hypothesis. The force of the radiation produced with the help of the hands corresponds fully to the force that one can expect from the "elastic cushion" between the palms, which Reich photographed on the X-ray film (Reich 1949d). The greater brilliance of the 25-watt and 15-watt bulbs compared with the bulbs of higher power, and the observation that the gas-filled bulbs seemed "damped" and "fogged," stand fully in accord with Reich's discovery that the pulse rates achievable in a vacuum are greater than those from gas-filled Geiger tubes: "Matter in the form of gas molecules or air molecules HINDERS the energy function of cosmic OR-energy, … Or-energy is strongest, or 'fastest,' in gas-free space" (Reich 1951f, p. 252).

www.orgonomie.net
                                         154

Emotional reactions
The radiation of the bulbs was connected with a series of specific emotional reactions that were remarkable. My own resistance, which delayed the observations for so long, has already been discussed. It can perhaps be generalized as: "Magic doesn't happen, and if it does, it will unsettle me. So I will only read about it." It seems clear that this is an example of the instinctive self-protection of the armored organism. In my case the irrationality was obvious, since it stood in direct contradiction to what I intellectually knew to be true and intellectually wanted to happen. But there was the fear of engaging with the real, living thing.


---


This fear became comprehensible to me after I felt the first great excitation while playing with the bulbs and had produced the luminous flashes and points and the radiation. It is very unsettling to recognize that the pestilential individual also reacts, indirectly through his armor, to this excitation. He senses that there is something in the whole undertaking that somehow is not "right." It is quite simple, for example, to make out a vague moralistic reaction behind the already-quoted dismissal of the A.E.I. manager. When one considers that stroking and rubbing the bulb is not unlike masturbation, the unconscious and implicit equation made through a distorted organ sensation can be better assessed: orgone = sex = something dirty, "improperly used." The remark, "Most people, of course, don't treat them like Aladdin's lamps" (which is essentially incomplete without the tone in which it was spoken), betrays the feeling that electric light bulbs are sold to be plugged into sockets and made to glow in order to see something—corresponding to the penis, which one has for the purpose of urinating. The hint that penises are organs of pleasure is downright shocking to many people, and likewise the suggestion that one can play pleasurably with electric light bulbs, simply because the light effects are beautiful and exciting to watch, is unconsciously felt as shocking. Perhaps Freudian lamp symbolism has deeper roots than we are aware of.

The term "excitation" is not used by chance for orgonotic radiation. The subjective enjoyment of the phenomenon is accompanied by an actual "charging up" of the organism and, as I judge it subjectively, of the atmosphere immediately surrounding it. Many of the observations were made while I sat in the orgone accumulator, in which I can normally sit for at least an hour without feeling any oppression. But on the evenings when I brought the bulb to radiation, I had to leave the accumulator twice because I had the feeling of having absorbed "too much" energy, of having received "too much" radiation from the bulb. This feeling of oppression was accompanied by a slight nausea, as described by Reich as symptomatic of overcharge. It improved immediately after a bath—the remedy that is recommended.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         155


---


The identity of the orgone energy within the body and the orgone energy outside the body was further evidenced by the anxiety reaction in a person to whom the light effects were shown. This person voiced concerns that I could damage my eyes, and admitted being afraid of the radiation. It seemed "eerie" and looked "like a witch's light." It is only a short way from this reaction to the fear that surrounds the feeling of energy moving in the body, and to the general tendency, in people who feel the currents, to believe that they are evil, that they must be the "promptings of the devil."70

On one of the evenings when I was observing the bulb, around 9 o'clock, as it was getting dark, a flash of light flickered across the sky. The identity of this flash in the sky with the lighting-up of the bulb was very striking. Emotionally and visually they seemed to be one and the same. This identity also makes sense in relation to Reich's assumption that lightning is a discharge of accumulated orgone, and it helped to throw some light on the peculiar phenomenon of the tiny spark visible on the contact pin of one of the bulbs, as well as the occurrence of electric current in the vacuum bulb, sufficient to make the neon lamp glow through the adapter plug. The possibility that the tungsten wire could function as a miniature lightning conductor, to divert part of the energy, is worth considering.

The comparison with lightning is confirmed by the second kind of lighting-up, the streak-like one, and by the fact that lightning, like St. Elmo's fire and will-o'-the-wisps, is something that even the mechanistic scientist has problems explaining. In a book about lightning it is noted that "at first sight it is odd that these great electrical machines (thunderclouds), which can develop potentials of thousands of millions of volts and produce sparks many miles long, consist only of water and ice blown along by the wind" (Schonland 1950, p. 122). In similar fashion Harvey attempts to explain will-o'-the-wisps: "Their nature is scarcely understood, but they have been attributed to burning phosphine, a spontaneously flammable gas supposedly produced somehow by the decomposition of organic material in the swamp. Unfortunately, phosphine is not known as a decomposition product of organic matter. Methane, a well-known degradation product, burns with a weak bluish flame, and some thought the will-o'-the-wisp was the result of this gas. Since methane does not ignite itself, how it catches fire remains to be explained" (Harvey 1940, p. 20).


---


Lightning, then, is "only water and ice blown away by the wind," and will-o'-the-wisps are just some nonflammable gases that somehow caught fire, and the radiation of vacuum lamps is "only statics," and "statics" is something that embarrasses the scientist; it is a nuisance, something he tries to get rid of, to kill off, to abolish; they are "atmospheric disturbances" that interfere with his radio, and "space waves" that disturb air traffic. And the underlying unity is completely lost.

70

www.orgonomie.net
                                        156

When I had first produced the lighting-up in the bulb and inquired whether this was an unknown or a well-known phenomenon, I could not believe that so easily produced an effect, in an everyday object like the household bulb, had escaped everyone's notice except scattered observers of the unusual. Then it seemed so obvious: here once again was the awe-inspiring fact that Reich has commented on—the thing is too simple. The taboo on putting one's hand to simple things is in full force; never touch it! As soon as you begin to sit in the dark, hard and hasty distinctions begin to dissolve, confusion arises, and things that could be counted on to happen no longer happen. You have to literally "grop[e]e in the dark" before a light goes on.

David Boadella: Orgonotic                                 71

Excitation Effects II (1958)
1. Some theoretical considerations
The radiation effects described in the last issue of this journal72 were repeated by a number of persons. I replied to the letter from the National Physical Laboratory (NPL), pointing out in expanded form the contradictions between their explanation and the observed behavior of the bulbs. A short reply was received, which repeats their conviction that the phenomena depend on "the type and pressure of the given residual gas, the composition of the glass of the bulb (since it influences the insulating properties and the ease of electrification), and probably on several other factors, such as the atmospheric humidity at the time." They close the subject by adding: "In our opinion there is little to be gained by pursuing the matter further, unless one decides … to [proceed] through carefully controlled experiments." The significance of the further experiments reported to them is ignored.


---


Two points of criticism were raised by a reader of the original article. The first concerns the vacuum pressure, and the second the charge of the hands. Both are of interest for the kind of complications that arise as soon as someone attempts to show that the light effects can be explained "simply enough" by orthodox physical concepts.

This person argued that, in accordance with the NPL's note, the less gas present, the lower the light yield. To support this view, she produced a 20-year-old vacuum lamp with a straight filament and challenged me to produce any light in it. She said that this lamp had a much higher vacuum than modern vacuum-filled lamps, and therefore did not glow.73 In further support of her view, she declared categorically that below a certain pressure, a vacuum does not glow at all, and that the pressure of 0.5 microns (stated in the article and used by Reich) was unattainable.

What one must note, in order to untangle the contradiction surrounding the vacuum question, is the contrast between the normal method of producing radiation in a

and Jean Ritter in Nottingham, England.

73
