with difficulty, or not at all."

www.orgonomie.net
                                         158

vacuum, and the results reported with the hands, which were confirmed by Dr. Ola Raknes with vacua whose pressure was known. Dr. Raknes obtained his results with vacua at a pressure of 1/10,000 and 1/100,000 of an atmosphere. Here is a description from a physics textbook of the normal course of the vacuum discharge:


---


When air is gradually pumped from a tube with two
                     plate electrodes maintained at a potential difference
                     of a few thousand volts, a luminous discharge appears
                     as the pressure approaches 1/1000 of an atmosphere.
                     Suppose we continue the evacuation of our discharge
                     tube. The luminous clouds shrink back from the middle
                     of the tube, and the dark space increases, until the
                     pressure in the tube is only one-millionth of an
                     atmosphere (i.e., 0.75 micron); the luminous
                     appearances in the gas have entirely ceased, although
                     electricity is still being conducted. The walls of the
                     tube glow with a greenish light. Invisible "rays"
                     penetrate the tube. These rays are a hail of electrons
                     moving with great velocity from the negative to the
                     positive pole. (Taylor 1936, p. 292)

With the 20-year-old bulb that had been handed to me, it proved very difficult to obtain any light with the hands. But it was easily made to glow by approaching it with a charged insulator of some kind. I did not find out what the pressure of this lamp is. The G.E.C. informed me, however, that the modern 25-watt bulbs are manufactured with a pressure of 0.0001 mm, i.e., 1 micron. Nevertheless, the ease of this radiation must be set against the NPL statement [National Physical Laboratory]: "From this, the fastest possible approach of a charged ebonite rod will produce no effect, since it is still not fast enough."


---


The second point of criticism from the same person was a direct denial that the charge of the skin is measurable in millivolts. She claimed that the hands could easily contain several thousand volts; therefore there was nothing remarkable about the radiation effects. She therefore saw no reason to accept the statement that the electrical voltage of the hands is a thousand times weaker than that used to produce the normal vacuum discharges, as quoted above. She would not accept Reich's oscillographic experiments, cited as evidence of the low voltage of the skin, since, as she said, "the oscillograph does not measure voltage, it measures current." The point is of some importance, for if one can show beyond doubt that the normal electrical voltage of the hands indeed lies in the range of millivolts, then it really becomes very difficult to understand how the gentlest stroking can lead to a radiation that requires thousands of volts. A look at Reich's report on his experiments made clear that it was voltage that he had measured. Reich connected the electrodes attached to the skin, by means of wires, to the grid plates in the electron tube of his oscillograph. An electron current flowed through these plates from a cathode to an anode. Any charge on the

www.orgonomie.net
                                         159

plates deflected the electron beam, and the deflection could be observed on a screen.

The oscillograph was calibrated at 10 millivolts. The observed deflection could therefore be compared directly with the calibration. The resistance of the oscillograph was 2 million ohms; the quantity of current produced by the hands was therefore extraordinarily low: Reich gives it as 0.000001 of an ampere, i.e., 1 microampere (Reich 1937).


---


In what follows, the distinction between the similarity of units and the equivalence of units, referred to in the first article, must constantly be kept in mind. It is then immaterial whether we use the term "statics" or "orgone" to describe certain effects, so long as we remember that "statics" is accompanied by properties that current-electricity does not possess. If one goes into a shop that sells electrical equipment or electrical measuring devices and asks for an electroscope, the typical reaction is "An electroscope? What is that? What is it used for?" Then perhaps a memory of secondary-school physics comes back, and they become dimly aware of what an electroscope is. The term "statics" emphasizes the similarities between effects with insulators and effects with metal and wires; the differences tend to be forgotten or overlooked—they are not important. The term "orgone" emphasizes the similarities between the reactions of insulators and the reactions of the living. The term "electricity" points to the phenomena connected with batteries and the movement of wires in magnetic fields. It is instructive to recall how the term came to be applied to insulators. Some of the earliest observations of "static" effects were made by Thales with pieces of amber. "Thales did not know why amber could attract light objects. He knew only that the amber had taken on a new peculiarity after being rubbed. We say that the amber was electrified, or that it acquired 'electricity' by rubbing. The word 'electricity' was chosen because the Greek name for amber is 'electron'! So when we say that a comb of ebonite or plastic has become electrically charged (electrified), we are actually saying that the comb has been 'amber-ized'; the comb behaves like amber when it is rubbed" (Dees 1945, p. 306).

It was the weighing of the differences between "statics" and electricity, and the similarities between "statics" and atmospheric orgone energy, that led Reich to the conclusion that "the concept of 'frictional electricity' could be replaced by that of orgonotic excitation! 'Frictional electricity' would then be nothing more than an uninteresting special case of orgonotic excitation, which could be based on passively absorbed orgone, or orgone that is radiated as part of living functioning" (Reich 1944, p. 115).

www.orgonomie.net
                                         160


---


2. The radiation of insulators
In the article on the glowing of light bulbs I described how a variety of materials were tried out, and how the use of these materials seemed to hinder the excitation by the hand. Since then, a number of exceptions have been found in which contact with certain materials produced a radiation just as strong—if not sometimes stronger—than that of the hands. I tried putting the 25-watt bulb into a rubber glove and pulling it out again. When both were dry, it glowed well. No vigorous rubbing was necessary: when the glove was held over the bulb and the fingers could move gently against it, there were also flashes in the bulb. With a little practice I could make the bulb glow faintly by moving it very close to the glove without touching it. On all these occasions the rubber glove was charged by first being crumpled in the palm for a few seconds, or by being stroked a few times over the hair.

Subsequently I tried using a Plexiglas cup and a strip of ethylene film (a plastic material used by designers). When the bulb was put into the cup and withdrawn, it lit up quite well. I tried to prevent the bulb from touching the sides of the cup. It touched them lightly at certain points, since the cup was simply not large enough to avoid that. But the actual contact was surely minimal. With the ethylene strip, the bulb lit up strongly at a distance of an inch or more when the strip was lightly moved. It may be recalled that the NPL managed to obtain a radiation of the bulb when Terylene was jerked past it. When a nylon stocking was pulled over my hand and the bulb was stroked through it, the glow was weaker than when the stocking was removed.

In the course of these experiments I noticed that the rubber glove lit up as I handled it, when the bulb was not being used. This again required no strong rubbing, but occurred only under dry conditions. It was possible to hold the glove at the lower end with the fingers hanging downward, and to stroke the radiation down toward the fingers. The light was again bluish, fairly weak, and sometimes easy to overlook. When I drew the glove evenly between two fingers, one could observe a clearly defined flickering bluish border along the edge of each finger. By this light one could, in an otherwise completely dark room, recognize both the position and the length of these two fingers, and also the angle between them, as the bluish border showed.


---


The observation that, when a silk or rayon garment is pulled vigorously over the head in the dark, it gives off bluish flashes, is not unusual, and is again casually explained as "electricity in your hair." I knew this effect very well in that context, but had never observed it in contact with my hands. I tried it with an old shirt of viscose fiber, rubbed between the hands. It flickered too, but

www.orgonomie.net
                                            161

somewhat more weakly than the rubber glove. The larger the surface involved, the stronger the effect seemed to be. When the front and back of the hand of the glove were pressed together, stroked, and then separated, it was possible to obtain fairly vigorous flashes. Rubbing the glove with the hand inside it produced no radiation at all when the glove was skin-tight. When it was lightly crumpled, so that a small gap existed between skin and glove, and the glove was then stroked, the folds radiated. Thus it was possible to vary the quantity of light produced, both through the type of material used and through the handling. It ranged from a weak glimmer of the glove when it was "crumbled" between the fingers (with an activity like crumbling tobacco), with only a very light movement of two fingertips, up to vigorous flashes when two larger flat surfaces were separated from one another.

Subsequently I tried out various kinds of plastic strips (old tablecloths, mackintoshes,74 etc.). I tried sitting in the orgone accumulator, with a plastic mac hanging next to the metal wall. When the mac was rubbed firmly against the metal once and then pulled toward me, there were strong flashes at the separation from the metal, and again when I brought my arm or hand close to the mac.75 When I held the back of my hand close to the strip of fabric, after it had been rubbed and pulled away in this manner, one could make out a tiny blue spark at each finger knuckle in turn. No feeling of discomfort occurred here, any more than with the tiny sparks obtained at the contact pins of the bulb, as described before.

The agreed opinion is that it takes 2,500 volts to produce an electric spark ½ mm long.

I tried other insulators—ebonite and glass. An ebonite rod drawn between the fingers gave a similar "shimmering" line to the rubber glove. I tried rubbing ordinary jam jars and got no effect, but a reader of the article telephoned to tell me that he obtained a lighting-up on the outside of glass cups when he rubbed them.


---


Around this time I read a letter in the News Chronicle from a woman complaining that there was too much "radioactivity," since her baby's plastic rattle glowed in a dark room with a "strange greenish light." She did not say whether this continued when the rattle was left untouched, but it seems likely that she observed the same kind of phenomenon in an insulator that had been excited by the baby. Two days later the following letter appeared in the News Chronicle, signed by a Fellow of the Institute of British Physicists, under the heading "Stored": "It is very unlikely that Baby Murkin's rattle is radioactive. Many substances glow in the dark due to a

release of energy that may have been stored in a chemical or physical form. Some, including some plastics, store energy when light falls on them, and release it slowly when they are in the dark.
John Vickers" (22.05.58).

In other words, the radiation is explained here by phosphorescence. The light excited the rattle (if it was the light, and not the handling, that provided the excitation), and as a result the rattle radiates. When we ask why it should radiate, the problem begins, for we find that with phosphorescence "there is not much that is less well understood." In the scientific sense, a phosphorescent object is something that, after being exposed to light, shines in the dark. Obviously it can store light and give it off again, but how this happens, no one knows" (Taylor 1936, p. 525). In a book on fluorescent lighting we read that "the various phenomena (of fluorescence in solids) are so complex that the explanations are not yet fully known" (Zwikker 1952b, p. 13).

Harvey, in his book on Living Light, has put forward some examples of radiation that seem to come closer to the present ones. His paragraph on it is interesting enough to quote in full:


---


What is often called frictional electricity is
                     actually electricity arising from the separation of
                     surfaces. Many examples are known. The transient
                     greenish luminescence that occurs at the point where
                     the electrician's or surgeon's tape, or "Scotch" tape,
                     is peeled from a roll has already been mentioned. The
                     author has observed that this luminescence can, with
                     some samples, be so bright that it is visible to only
                     partially dark-adapted eyes. The phenomenon can be
                     repeated when the tape is rewound and then peeled off
                     again, and also appears when the sticky sides of the
                     tape are pressed together and then separated from one
                     another. Mounting adhesives, whether two pieces of
                     metal, glass, paper, cellophane, or any two different
                     materials, luminesce when the surfaces are separated.
                     Many other substances, when clinging closely together,
                     will also luminesce when pulled apart. Films that are
                     stripped from glass or metal give a flash of
                     luminescence—for example, collodion dissolved in an
                     ether-alcohol mixture, poured onto a glass plate and
                     allowed to dry. (Harvey 1940, p. 105f)

Harvey goes on to give his explanation for these effects:

The explanation for all such luminescences seems
                     to be the following: whenever two surfaces are
                     separated from one another, the capacitance decreases
                     and the voltage rises until a discharge takes place,
                     whereby the

www.orgonomie.net
                                         163

surrounding gas is excited to luminesce. That a
                     discharge actually takes place can easily be shown by
                     stripping adhesive tape or Scotch tape in a neon-gas
                     atmosphere of 2 to 4 cm Hg pressure. The luminescence
                     is then reddish … Red luminescence also occurs
                     when two strips of mica are pulled apart, or when
                     collodion or amber-like or rubber films are stripped
                     from glass in a low-pressure neon atmosphere. (Harvey
                     1940, p. 105)

So we are brought back to volts, and to the kind of sentence that characterizes the letter from the NPL: the explanation "seems" to be this or that. The nuance, or gentleness, of the activity necessary to produce a radiation is completely incompatible with the electrical conception of volts.


---


At this point two further remarkable observations must be recorded. I charged an Ethilon strip [a kind of nylon] by drawing it through my fingers, and noticed the flickering that arose when it seemed that some of the points of light lay outside the area of the Ethilon. At first it looked as if a part of the light were being "flung" into the adjacent space, as if I had dipped a cloth in phosphorescent paint and shaken off part of the paint. But on closer inspection it turned out that the points of light were in fact briefly glowing at the edge of a nearby wooden shelf. By further repetition of the charging of the Ethilon, this could be repeated many times. At some point I could produce a long row of points of light at the edge of the shelf when I stroked over the Ethilon in its vicinity. The wood was not touched at all; no friction was exerted, and there was no separation of surfaces. It was also possible to evoke tiny points of light on the back of the hand, in the same way—without contact and without spark formation. The emotional impression of these glowing points, appearing in dead, motionless wood and in an inactive hand, was very moving. The points on the wood in particular, which were more numerous, reminded me of a cloudy night sky in which a cloud has for a time grown thinner, so that the stars shine through for a second before being covered over again the next moment.

The second observation was first made when the plastic film was pulled away from the accumulator wall, as described above. As I held my arm nearby to induce the radiation, I felt on the back of my arm a very pronounced and unmistakable feeling of warmth and tingling. Most remarkable and most convincing is the fact that this prickling feeling is exactly what one gets from the accumulator wall when the arm is held at a small distance from it. The main difference is that the effect of the insulator is much stronger as long as it lasts, being especially pronounced at a distance of six inches or more from the material, but quickly subsiding, whereas the sensations induced by the accumulator wall are continuous and vary only with the weather.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         164

I have also easily obtained this effect outside the accumulator, and it was strongest with the Ethilon strip. The sensation can best be described as that connected with very light contact with fur, or with threads that one has drawn over the skin. The accumulator sensations have also been compared with this kind of effect (Boadella, Fancroff 1955).


---


I tried to find out whether there is any reference in the literature on static electricity to such an effect. Unfortunately most modern textbooks are occupied with the quantitative and mathematical description of the static field, and ignore its qualitative properties. It was necessary to delve into books written in the last century before I had the good fortune to find the following brief note:

Other properties of electrified bodies – Exp. 67.
                     Electrify a glass rod strongly and hold it near the
                     face. A peculiar sensation will be perceived,
                     comparable to that of contact with cobwebs. (Poyser
                     1895, p. 59)

When I came out of the accumulator, after having sat in it for about an hour with a plastic film loosely attached to the inner walls, my wife exclaimed in surprise that I looked very red. I looked at myself in the mirror and saw that my face was indeed much redder than usual, and my neck was blotchy, as if I had a light rash. This soon subsided. Bioenergetic reactions like these are known to users of the accumulator in milder form. They cannot in the least be explained by such remarks as: "You just sit in there and it gets hot and stuffy" (Washington Daily Post, 28.09.56). The presence of a radiation of some kind, emanating from the excited plastic or Ethilon strip, is unmistakable. And this radiation has all the properties that we associate with orgone.

www.orgonomie.net
                                           165

3. The radiation of the electroscope
To advance the observations on insulators a step further, I acquired two gold-leaf electroscopes. The electroscopes were not expensive, and the brass rods that held the leaves were simply enclosed in glass bottles with rubber stoppers. They were bought at a time when I was rubbing everything that was made of glass, to see whether it lit up, so that on the evening they were delivered, as soon as it got dark, I rubbed the electroscope with some care, since I did not want to damage or twist the sensitive gold leaf. After a minute or two, a bluish-green radiation appeared intermittently around the belly of the bottle, and less pronounced in the neck of the bottle. Neither of the radiations was particularly strong, but unmistakable nonetheless. Occasionally there was a stronger small flash in the middle of the container, where I assumed the leaves were located.


---


When the same stroking of the neck and belly of the bottle was carried out in daylight, I noticed no radiation, but found—at first with some surprise—that the leaves could in fact be made to diverge very easily in this way. On a dry day it was possible, with one or two strokes carried out with the hand over the glass, to produce a very strong divergence. The structural similarity between the electroscope and the electric light bulb struck me for the first time at this point. Each consists of a glass envelope surrounding a kind of metal rod. Whether this arrangement has anything to do with the ease with which the charge of the hand passes through the glass in each case, I do not know.

The fact that the electroscope, like the light bulb, responds directly to excitation by the hand is, at first glance, remarkable, since touching the electroscope disc with the hand is the usual way of discharging the electroscope. The idea of charging the electroscope by hand sounds odd, exactly like the idea of making a light bulb radiate with the hand. Electroscopes are not intended to be used in this way. In fact, the more expensive electroscopes, with a wooden box and only two sides of flat glass standing at right angles to the plane of the leaves, could probably not be so easily charged by hand. The strongest reactions in the electroscope, as in the light bulb, are found when it is possible to encompass the glass envelope, so to speak creating an exciting enclosure.76 Nevertheless, the electroscope is only meant to be charged at the disc, just as light bulbs are "meant" to be lit from the socket through the filament.

A further preliminary objection can be formulated: gas discharge should be attributable to the ionization of the gas atoms. We have seen that stroking the electric bulb produces a gas discharge, regarded by the classical physicist as a consequence of the ionization of a part of the gas by "hand-produced electrification." According to the ionization theory, the ionization occurs the more easily the more gas is present. Therefore we can

76
  Similarly, orgone, when used therapeutically, works most effectively in the form
of an enclosure. The orgone cushion, for example, is much stronger when it is wrapped
around the affected part of the body, instead of simply being laid on it.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         166


---


expect that the air in electroscopes, at atmospheric pressure, glows more easily than the gas filling in bulbs, which stand under a lower pressure. In fact very little radiation was observed in the air of the electroscope: it was mainly the side of the glass and the metal that glowed. Nevertheless, the "electrification" must have gone through the glass in order to deflect the leaves. It is also known that a charged electroscope can discharge faster in proportion to the degree of ionization of the surrounding air. Thus air radiates when it is excited through the glass of a bulb because it is "ionized." When, however, the same action is carried out with an electroscope, which likewise radiates and is thereby charged, the discharging effect of this "ionized air" is overlooked. We shall soon find a similar contradiction in connection with the grounding of the electroscope.

Before I describe further experiments with the electroscope, I must mention a very strong resistance that developed over the course of several weeks. I knew that everything that happened with the electroscope would be explained by the orthodox physicist as positive and negative electricity. Mechanical rubbing was supposed to lead, in some insulators, to a surplus of electrons (negative charge), and in others to a deficiency of electrons (positive charge). Over the course of many weeks, in which I brought various materials up to the electroscope in different combinations to see how it reacted, I had this orthodox explanation of the phenomena in my head. The behavior of the electroscope was very confusing: sometimes it reacted in a way that seemed to contradict the orthodox bipolar theory; at other times it seemed to confirm it. Whenever that happened, I was tempted to leave the whole thing be, to bow to the greater knowledge of the electrophysicists, and to think that after all it must all just be "electricity." My doubts were reinforced by the fact that, after I had read Reich's article on orgone and statics, I found that Reich had misrepresented the views of the electrophysicist on one or two points. Something in me wanted to hold on to the certainty of not having to ask questions or solve problems, if I accepted the electrical explanation without further procrastination. The prospect of being out there, with only a few groundbreaking orgone-physical insights to guide me, was frightening. I began to feel what the well-disposed electrophysicist in Reich's article describes: "I would not have thought that a simple electroscope could make one rack one's brain so much."


---


I was also afraid of making mistakes, of making a fool of myself in the eyes of people who possessed far more knowledge of physics than I, and of putting my foot in it generally. I noticed that it takes a certain courage simply to say, as Reich did: "I would like to ask the reader to be indulgent regarding small errors that might be found here and there. When one fights one's way through a jungle, one easily stumbles over a root and makes a mistake. The pioneer in the jungle does not necessarily have to know the exact chemical composition of the leaves. Theoretical physics contains so many fundamental errors that it cannot afford to appear in the role of the intolerant critic of a young and pioneering, fruitful science such as orgone physics" (Reich 1944, p. 98).

www.orgonomie.net
                                         167

4. Attraction and dissociation with insulators
What was so disconcerting about the differences between the orgone-physical and the academic explanations of the electroscope reactions was that they should be so easily resolved through careful observations; yet when I came to careful observations, the difficulties did not decrease in the least, but rather increased. The electrical explanation says simply, and with the dogmatism of a physical law, that "like charges repel one another, unlike charges attract one another." If one charges an electroscope positively and brings up a negatively charged rod, the leaves should therefore converge. If one takes a second positively charged rod, the leaves should diverge all the more.

Usually rubber and glass are taken as the materials that, when rubbed with suitable materials, take on a negative and a positive charge respectively. It should be a very simple matter to charge the electroscope with rubber and then take some charged glass and see what happens. I have done this on many occasions. Sometimes the leaves converged, and the electrical theory of positive and negative electricity seemed completely proven. But at other times the leaves diverged all the more, exactly as Reich had described. Thus both the electrophysicist and the orgonomist seem to be right "in a certain way." I tried to find out whether I was being unfair, or whether I was somehow prejudicing the results. I carefully checked whether the electroscope had in fact retained its positive charge before I took the negative one, and vice versa.


---


I laid the electroscope aside with the inner feeling that it must somehow be "faulty" and "playing crazy," and began experimenting with various insulating materials. I chose mainly the materials that I had found well suited: glass, rubber, polyethylene film, ebonite, Plexiglas, and ethylene. I charged each of these materials by drawing them through my hands or leading them over my hair, and confirmed that they were indeed charged by holding them over the electroscope disc to see whether the leaves moved, and then establishing whether they repelled or attracted one another.

The Ethilon strip, when freely suspended and undisturbed by air currents, was strongly attracted by charged polyethylene, rubber, ebonite, and glass, but repelled by Plexiglas. Conclusion, according to the electrical theory: Ethilon and Plexiglas are charged alike; antithetical to them, but charged alike in relation to one another, are polyethylene film, rubber, ebonite, and glass. Yet we know that rubber and glass should have opposite charges, and therefore Ethilon should be repelled either by rubber or by glass.

When a piece of charged rubber was suspended, it was attracted by Plexiglas, Ethilon, and glass; and repelled by ebonite, polythene, and another piece of rubber. Conclusion according to the electrical theory: glass has the same charge as

www.orgonomie.net
                                        168

Ethilon and Plexiglas. But we have already found that glass attracts Ethilon, and should therefore be of opposite charge.

When a thin polyethylene film was freely suspended, it was repelled by rubber and ebonite, but attracted by glass, ethylene, and Plexiglas. The matter now seems to be starting to become simple: glass must be of the same charge as Ethilon and Plexiglas, and the fact that glass attracts Ethilon must be an "error." In the course of the ever-renewed repetition of these observations, I found that I could get the charged Plexiglas to attract the charged Ethilon, then repel it, and after that attract it again. It was exactly as if the material had a head of its own and was deliberately unpredictable, to confuse me. Thus ethylene and Styrofoam sometimes behave as if they were charged alike, and sometimes as if of opposite charge. In one moment they are positive, and in the next negative.


---


What I observed was exactly parallel to the behavior of glass and rubber at the electroscope; sometimes an effect in one direction, and sometimes in the opposite direction. The more I tried to "nail down" the material and make it behave unambiguously in one direction or the other, the more I had to recognize that this process is not rigid, and cannot be reduced to the question of whether particular materials are regularly left with too few or too many electrons when they are rubbed. In fact, the theory of positive and negative electricity, as applied to the attraction and repulsion of insulators, is tenable only if one overlooks and ignores the occasions when rubber and glass mutually reinforce their excitation of the electroscope, and the occasions when apparently identically charged materials attract one another.

Subsequently I could pick up a smaller piece of Ethilon by holding the large strip about an inch above it. The smaller piece then sprang upward and stuck, exactly as one would expect if the two pieces had opposite charges. But both pieces came from one original strip, both had been rubbed in the same way, and both had in fact first shown, by acting on the electroscope, that they were charged.

Similar considerations led Reich to the conclusion that the phenomena could be better explained by assuming that the insulators are charged with one and the same energy, but that it exhibits two antithetical functions: attraction and dissociation (or repulsion). "Orgone energy thus does not consist of two opposite fluids, but of two antithetical functions, attraction and repulsion; and each of these functions has a specific relation to the nature of the substance" (Reich 1944, p. 136). This conclusion is not so revolutionary as it sounds. When we seek the origin of the term positive and negative electricity, as used for insulators, we find:

The electrification of glass rubbed with silk was
                     formerly called vitreous (from vitrum, the Latin word
                     for glass); that derived from sealing wax or resin
                     rubbed with flannel was called resinous.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         169

These names, however, have long since been
                     displaced by others, since glass electricity could be
                     obtained from substances that produced resinous
                     electricity, and vice versa, merely by changing the
                     material of the rubber. (Poyser 1895, p. 60)


---


It is known that when rough glass is rubbed on smooth glass, the two pieces of glass show attraction, and should therefore be of opposite charge. The state of the substance, the manner in which it is excited, and the intensity of its initial charge are all factors that determine whether it is the attracting or the dissociative function that is expressed.

After this description of the excitation of one insulator by another, it is easier to understand the activity of the electroscope leaves.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         170

5. Charging and discharging the electroscope
This activity is of a threefold kind: we can distinguish the way the electroscope charges, the way it discharges, and the way the charged leaves move when excited. The following description presupposes no prior knowledge of electroscope reactions. It seemed best to describe all the observed phenomena accurately and faithfully—the known and familiar, as well as the unknown and the ignored. In this way the entire pattern of electroscope reactions can emerge more clearly.

A routine procedure for charging an electroscope consists in taking a well-insulating material, rubbing it vigorously several times on a suitable fabric, and then bringing it near the metal disc of the electroscope. Glass rubbed on silk, leather, or felt is a common choice, likewise ebonite rubbed on wool or flannel. As the rubbed material approaches the brass disc, the leaves of the electroscope diverge. If the charged material is then removed without having touched the disc, the leaves converge again: the excitation ceases. If, however, the insulator can come into contact with the disc, the charge is retained when the insulator is removed. Whether this happens immediately after contact, or whether repeated contacts or a longer contact are required, seems to depend on the kind of material used and on the humidity at the time.

A second method is known as "charging by induction." The insulator is brought near the disc without touching it and held there, while the disc is "grounded" by touching it with the finger. The finger is removed, and then the insulator: the leaves of the electroscope converge when the finger touches the disc, and diverge when the finger and the insulator are removed.

A third procedure for charging the electroscope by stroking the neck of the bottle with the hand has already been described, and will be discussed in more detail later.

A fourth method was first described by Reich in 1939 (see Reich 1939). His three experiments are of the greatest importance, since they led directly to the construction of the first orgone accumulator, to enclose the energy whose effects he was observing.


---


In brief, Reich found by chance that a pair of rubber gloves left lying near some of his bion preparations deflected the leaves of a nearby electroscope. He checked whether the gloves had not become charged in some other way. Subsequently he was able to achieve the same charging of the electroscope by bringing uncharged gloves into contact with the abdomen or genitals of a vegetatively mobile person, or by exposing them to strong solar radiation for a certain time. A few years later (1944) Reich succeeded in charging the electroscope with rubber that had lain in an orgone accumulator for several days (see Reich 1944). Those critics of Reich who praise his psychiatric and sociological work, but judge his

www.orgonomie.net
                                         171

physics quite differently, would do well to remember that the experiment with the rubber on the genitals or the abdomen developed, like so many of his other experiments, directly out of his psychiatric and sex-economic work. These three experiments with the electroscope were the first orgone-physical experiments ever carried out.

I have attempted to repeat these experiments and to charge the electroscope as Reich described. The most difficult thing, with the first pair of gloves I used, was to carry out the control experiment—holding the uncharged glove near the electroscope and obtaining no deflection. Contrary to expectation, a glove that I had no intention of exciting by any of the described means nevertheless produced a deflection of the leaves. It seemed that the mere handling of the glove, without the intention of exciting it, somehow left a strong charge in the glove. As long as I continued to handle the glove while bringing it near the electroscope disc, the charge persisted. Only immersion in water removed it completely, and holding the gloves with tweezers proved to be the only successful method of eliminating charges emanating from the hand. When the rubber glove was left on the hair for a quarter of an hour and then removed very carefully, so as to make really any rubbing negligible, a strong charge was still shown at the electroscope. This is in accord with the ease with which a Plexiglas rod can be charged by a single stroke over the hair of the head (see Reich 1944 and the letter from Dr. Raknes [presented following this chapter]). The experiment of charging the glove through contact with the skin of the abdomen was also satisfactorily repeated. It was not possible to repeat the experiment with radiation in the sun or in the accumulator, because of the persistent rainy and humid weather in Nottingham that summer.


---


I bought a second pair of gloves, which showed no reaction at all at the electroscope, no matter what treatment they received. The material was described as "satinized" rubber, but why this glove would not react at all is still a riddle. Possibly it has something to do with the fact that not all rubber products are insulators. The question of which chemical components, manufacturing processes, and energy states are connected with such great contrasts, in materials that outwardly differ so little from one another, seems to require intensive research. At present very little is known about it.

I tried grounding the electroscope disc, to see whether this would make charging more difficult or impossible. With grounding there was no reaction to a charged insulator brought near the disc or laid on it. But the grounding did not prevent the charging produced by stroking the glass with the hand. It was also possible to stroke over the rubber insulation of the wire used for grounding, and in this way to evoke a divergence of the leaves. This last fact shows most clearly the antithesis between orgone and electricity. Rubber insulators are used by the electrician to prevent electricity from flowing

www.orgonomie.net
                                         172

from the wire outward. The same rubber insulation, however, facilitates the flow of orgone from outside inward to the wire. The objection that the "voltage" of the hands is high enough to pass through the rubber insulation merely raises the unsolved problem of how this "voltage" gets into the hands in the first place. It is also refuted by Reich's discovery that the orgone flow in insulators is more effective the better the insulation: "The better the insulator, the more pronounced the reaction—Styrofoam is known as an excellent insulator. There was always this reaction" (Reich 1944, p. 119).

The routine procedure for discharging the electroscope consists in touching the disc with the hand or with metal, i.e., "grounding" the leaves. We may not, however, assume that what happens necessarily has anything to do with grounding. A series of observations contradicts this assumption.

On one or two occasions I had great difficulty discharging the electroscope after it had been charged with the hands on the glass of the bottle. Metal or my hand on the disc had no effect. Only immersing three-quarters of the electroscope glass in water brought the leaves to discharge on such occasions.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         173


---


6. The pulsation of the electroscope leaves
So far, various ways of charging and discharging the electroscope have been described. What follows is a report on the behavior of the electroscope after it has been charged: When I removed the material that had charged the electroscope at the disc (either by direct contact or by induction), the leaves first began to converge, then almost immediately to diverge again; sometimes the extent of the re-divergence was greater than that of the initial divergence. Often it was somewhat less. And at other times it was slight, or did not occur at all. In the latter case the electroscope could not maintain the charge transferred to it. It is interesting that when the electroscope retained or even increased its initial charge, this never took place directly after the removal of the charging material. There was always an intermediate phase of partial convergence of the leaves. The faster I removed the insulator, the faster the leaves jerked together and apart again. It was even possible to miss the reaction if one did not look specifically for it, just as one is not aware of how often a person blinks with their eyelids, unless the blinking is very obvious, or one is out to notice precisely this. The analogy to blinking may sound far-fetched, but it is a fascinating fact that the excited gold leaves behave in many ways as if they were "alive." Reich observed a similar attraction and repulsion in excited silk threads. He wrote that "the reaction reminds me of contracting frog legs. At first I wanted to dismiss this comparison" (Reich 1944, p. 134). The translator of the article, Theodor Wolfe, added an interesting footnote:

It seems strange to me that the movement of a pair
                      of silk threads should recall a biological movement.
                      I remember well my astonishment when I saw this
                      experiment for the first time. My immediate impression
                      was in fact that of moving frog legs. To be a witness of
                      such experiments, which again and again demonstrate the
                      functioning of a unified energy in both the physical and
                      the biological realm, is one of the most impressive
                      experiences. (Reich 1944, p. 134)


---


The phenomenon of alternating movement as a reaction to the excitation of a charged electroscope occurs not only on the removal of the insulator, but also on the renewed approach of the same insulator. That is, if one brings a piece of charged rubber near an electroscope that has already been excited by the same piece of rubber and has retained its charge, the leaves begin to converge as the rubber approaches, but from a certain point they diverge again and remain diverged when the rubber rests on the disc. Thus, by carrying out two movements with the charged insulator (toward the electroscope disc and away from it), I produced four alternating movements in the leaves: convergence, divergence, convergence, divergence. It was the observation of this kind of movement, here and in other contexts, that led Reich to use the term "pulsation" to describe what had taken place.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         174

Reich pointed out that the pulsation in the leaves "occurs only when the contact of the energy field with the excited substance is established, and when it is interrupted" (Reich 1944, p. 133). The same phenomenon is found when a bulb is excited by a charged insulator at a distance. Reich described how the flickering of a fluorescent lamp excited by a charged rod corresponds to a sequence of "contact and contact-interruption." It also seems probable that this happens when we stroke a bulb with our hands. Harvey's emphasis on the "separation of surfaces" as an explanation of the luminescence of adhesive tape, etc., can now be regarded as a special case of "contact-interruption." The contact of surfaces is not essential, since the attraction and dissociation of energy fields leads both to radiation effects in bulbs and insulators and to pulsation effects in electroscope leaves.

The phenomena are far from submitting to a rigid, or hard and fast, schematization. The twofold movement of pulsation (convergence-divergence) is by no means the only reaction to excitation at the disc. Every kind of movement also occurs in isolation. Sometimes there is only convergence, sometimes only a stronger divergence. That these differences cannot be explained with "positive and negative electricity" follows from the fact that the approach of one and the same charged material to an identically charged electroscope can lead to either of these opposite movements. The assumption of attraction and dissociation as antithetical functions of a unified energy makes better sense here, in light of the facts, as shown by the effects of insulators on one another.


---


So far the excitation of the leaves by an insulator has been described. Very slight excitation by my hand was observed, in that the leaves reacted with minimal movement when my hand approached the disc. If, however, one placed a charged insulator on the electroscope disc (e.g., by draping it with the soft rubber glove), the convergence of the leaves was much more pronounced. That is, the hand, which cannot influence an uncharged electroscope at the disc, can produce a considerable movement of the charged leaves when it is moved about 6 inches above the disc—provided only that the metal disc is covered by the insulator. The insulator seems to "amplify" the field effect of the hand. Reich was early confronted with the fact that the electroscope did not respond directly, by charging, to an orgone concentration. It was charged only when the orgone had been absorbed and subsequently emitted by a good insulator. A similar indirectness seems to govern the charging effect of the hands. The hands cannot charge an electroscope at the disc, but they will excite rubber or glass or other insulators, which in turn have a strong effect on the electroscope.

This indirect action of the hands on the gold leaves is seen most clearly when the leaves are charged by stroking the glass of the bottle. When I took my hands away from the bottle, the leaves first began slowly to converge and then slowly to diverge again. In other words, the already-mentioned pulsation, produced by insulators, also occurs as a

www.orgonomie.net
                                         175

reaction to the hands. The same phenomenon of slowness, when the charging occurred via an insulator, in comparison with direct metallic conduction, was observed by Reich (see Reich 1944, p. 119).

When my hands approached the neck of the bottle again, there was a very strong movement of the leaves. Both convergence on approach and divergence on approach were observed, and both still occurred even when all precautions had been taken to ensure that the manner of stroking was identical. It was possible to charge two electroscopes in the same way and obtain a divergence in one and a convergence in the other, by the same hand, approached in the same manner.


---


Two further unusual observations were made with the electroscope charged in this way. When I laid my finger on the disc—before the already-described slow re-divergence had taken place, and while the slow convergence on removing the hand from the glass was still going on—there was a very sudden outward "jolt" in the leaves. In other words, the normally slow reaction to excitation by the hand through the glass was greatly accelerated. It was indeed possible to deflect the leaves by an angle of about 30° from the vertical position by laying my finger on the disc. The hand, which normally discharges the electroscope at the disc, is here able to charge the electroscope at the disc. Not only can it be shown that "grounding" sometimes has no effect on discharging the electroscope; rather, the mere procedure of "grounding" under these conditions will produce exactly the opposite of what normally results from it. Thus it is not surprising when one finds that metal, when brought onto the disc, produces the same reaction.

Also, when the electroscope was partially charged by stroking the glass and then lifted up from the table, a marked increase in the divergence of the leaves was noticed. This not in the form of a "jolt," but of a fairly rapid steady widening of the leaves. This confused me for quite a while, since the deflection occurred only on an upward movement, but when I held the electroscope in free space and moved it up or down, no change occurred. Then it became clear that this seemingly bizarre deflection was produced only when the contact between the glass and the tabletop was interrupted. This could not be a mere question of the separation of surfaces, since the reaction occurred only when the electroscope bulb had first been stroked. It was the dissociation of the two energy fields that excited the leaves: stroking the glass vessel also excites the tabletop, whether it is made of smooth wood or covered with a metal plate or plastic tablecloth—all variants that were tried and in which the reaction occurred.

When the belly of the bottle was stroked instead of the neck, the leaves reacted with strong excitation. It was possible to simulate the effect of a strong breeze within the airtight bottle and to obtain a complete jerking of the leaves with a deflection of 90 degrees or more. When this stroking was carried out slowly on one side of the belly of the bottle,

www.orgonomie.net
                                         176


---


with the bottle held horizontally in the other hand, the near leaf could be made to extend toward my hand. Again the comparison with a biological movement—an erection—was felt by several observers of the phenomenon. The "erection" of the gold leaf followed a pattern that now seems gradually to be typical: it happened sometimes as a reaction to the approach of my hand (with collapse on removal), and sometimes to the removal of my hand (with collapse on approach).

The strongest field effect was found when the electroscope was charged by my hands and a rubber glove, likewise charged by my hands, was moved back and forth alongside the glass. Grounding the disc now eliminated the reaction just as little as it had previously. The leaves reacted to the movement of the glove with a movement of their own, which diminished with increasing distance of the glove. A tiny but unmistakable increase in the deflection of the leaves could be observed at a distance of two and a half feet. With the Ethilon strip, this distance could be exceeded by about a further foot.

The electroscope jerks in response to "something" in the excited rubber glove across a distance of 2 or 3 feet. Little is gained by calling this "something" an electrostatic field. We have seen that it can be produced without friction. It can lead to reddening and prickling of the skin. It can produce radiation in neon lamps, as well as in light bulbs. It excites pieces of wood, which glow with brightly shining points of light. It can be obtained from contact with the hair or with the skin. "The electroscope is not charged with current, but with orgone. Orgone penetrates everything—conductors and non-conductors—only at different speeds. The insulator conducts no electricity, but it conducts orgone. For this reason one can charge an electroscope with an orgonotically charged insulator, just as one can discharge the electroscope through an insulator" (Reich 1944, p. 120).

It is easy to understand why the electroscope is of only slight use to workers in the field of electricity, whereas it constitutes a fundamental instrument of orgonomy. This was what led Reich to rename the instrument and to designate it more fittingly an "orgonometer." Only the assumption that it is orgone, and not electricity, that excites the insulators and the gold leaves makes it possible to understand the functional relations between light here and charge there; between the obvious vegetative reaction on the one side and the subtle emotional reaction on the other. We can investigate these relations further in the excitation induced by the orgone energy field measuring device.

Bernd Laska: The Orgone Accumulator
             78

(1976)
1. Introduction
The most adventurous rumors have always circulated about the orgone accumulator (hereafter: accumulator), regardless of whether access to serious information about it was in each case easy or difficult. In their majority, these rumors recognizably serve the function of fending off Reich's theories as a whole (and here especially those of sexuality), as do the rumors about his person, and the often conspicuously foregrounded interest in discussing whether Reich had "really gone mad." I presumably need not give examples of this; they will be familiar to most readers from their own experience.

In what follows, therefore, everything essential for a potential user is to be compiled in somewhat compressed form about the accumulator, since the information in Reich's writings published in German to date is not detailed enough in this respect.

2. History of its origin
The accumulator was not a brilliant idea that Reich suddenly had, but the result of years of intensive research work.

After a good decade of clinical work on a psychoanalytic foundation, Reich had reached, around 1932/33, the limits of psychology. Certainly there were (and still are today) details to be researched; but that was not a task for the pioneer Reich—he did not want to research in breadth, but in depth. Since Reich's orientation was from the beginning a natural-scientific one, he kept himself informed, alongside his other activities, about developments in other sciences such as biology, medicine, physiology, etc. Moreover, with his insights into the causes of the origin of neuroses, his sociopolitical engagement grew, so that one could hardly expect Reich to spend his life, like most psychoanalysts, in the lucrative curing of neuroses.

The essential findings that had been won with the psychoanalytic method, Reich maintained against the "revisionist" endeavors of many analysts and of Freud himself, and brought them into connection with findings that came from other natural sciences. He integrated selected results from physiology, biochemistry (especially colloid chemistry), and biology, which he combined with his own

78
  Using the publications of Wilhelm Reich and material made available by Manfred
Hinz (Munich) and Rony Werams (Stuttgart). Reprinted with the kind permission of
the author. From: Wilhelm Reich Blätter 4/76.

www.orgonomie.net
                                         180


---


character-analytic findings, seeking to develop them into a holistic theory of the human being. Thus, for example, the concept of the vegetative current comes from the Berlin internist Friedrich Krause. The investigations of Walter and Käthe Misch on the biochemistry of pleasure and anxiety, as well as L.R. Müller's research on the vegetative nervous system, complemented one another in Reich's view, and with his own concepts. During this year of intensive study (1934) in Danish exile, Reich himself made no new discoveries; he processed the findings mentioned and further results of the specialized sciences—hardly ever before brought into relation with one another—together with his own theories, into the fundamental biological concept of "psychosomatic identity and antithesis." In this process of working through all the theories that seemed relevant to him in this connection, questions soon arose whose answering he had to take on himself. So he brushed up his old experimental knowledge, extended it, and, with a few specially trained coworkers, attacked these new problems. Thus came about the electrophysiological series of experiments on anxiety and sexuality (long before Masters/Johnson, and with a different aim), and the bion experiments.

Despite the poor external conditions of emigration, the years 1933-39 were a very fruitful period in Reich's life. In the ideological sphere too, a decisive change took place in these years. Through the close collaboration with the French vitalist Roger DuTeil in the bion experiments, and under the impression of developments in the "socialist camp," Reich slowly detached himself from official Marxism, without, however, giving up the goals that had once led him and many others to Marxism.

In his electrophysiological experiments and in the work with the bion cultures, Reich was still working with the concept of bioelectricity. Some results, however, did not fit in there at all, so that Reich found himself compelled to postulate the existence of an energetic radiation outside the electromagnetic spectrum, which he called orgone energy.

This so consistent path of Reich's, which could only be sketched here in the very roughest outline, is generally little known, and is therefore placed before the actual topic here.

3. Functional principle of the orgone accumulator
The accumulator functions on the basis of the following properties of orgone energy, which Reich determined in extensive series of experiments:

1) Orgone energy fills the entire space, penetrates all matter, but different substances at different speeds. It is thus present everywhere, merely in different densities or concentrations. Accumulators therefore function everywhere.

2) Material that is a good insulator, or a poor conductor, for electrical energy attracts orgone energy. Good conductors (as a rule, therefore, metals) likewise attract orgone, but immediately repel it again.

www.orgonomie.net
                                             181


---


In Reich's writings, "organic" or "non-metallic" material is also often used for insulator. Concretely, galvanized sheet steel is used today as the metal (aluminum sheet has a harmful effect!) and steel wool, and as the insulator, insulating board and mineral wool (not cotton wool, since it attracts water!).

The accumulator consists, in principle, of a box closed on all sides, whose walls consist of any number of double layers of electrically conductive and insulating material, arranged so that the metal layer lies on the inside in each case. For details, see the construction instructions.

The effectiveness of an accumulator depends on the following parameters:

o   Type of insulator material: e.g., plastic is better than wood
             o   Type of metal: for medical purposes only iron or steel
             o   Level of relative atmospheric humidity, because water absorbs orgone: the drier the better
             o   Geographical latitude: the nearer the equator the better
             o   Elevation above sea level: the higher the better
             o   Number of layers: the more the better. But effectiveness does not increase linearly with the number of layers, as was established by electroscope measurements. Thus a single-layer (i.e., with one double layer) accumulator gave a concentration 3 times as high as in the atmosphere, but a 3-layer accumulator had only 4 times the concentration, and a 10-layer one only 6 times—thus only twice as strong as the single-layer one.
             o   Distance of the body from the inner wall during a session: the nearer the better, but do not touch!
             o   Number of accumulators in a room or building: the more there are, the higher the general orgone concentration already is, and thus also the accumulation.

The concentration of orgone energy in the accumulator is objectively demonstrable by measuring the temperature difference between the upper interior of the accumulator and the surroundings. This effect was at the time first confirmed by Einstein, but then (probably because of its fundamental shattering of the theoretical edifice of physics) explained away conventionally—unjustly, as Reich believes and has documented.79

Further possibilities for objectifying orgone through measurements on the accumulator exist with the electroscope and the Geiger-Müller counter. Details of this would, however, go too far within the scope of this article.

4. Construction instructions
(a) The following have offered to advise on the construction of an accumulator: Manfred Hinz (…) Rony Werams (…)

5. Notes on the use of the accumulator
The accumulator should stand in a ventilated place, where, however, it must be
protected from moisture. In atmospherically unfavorable locations, such as large
cities, the inner surfaces (the sheet steel) should be wiped about once a week with
a damp cloth and then dried off, in order to remove any deposits resulting from
DOR (…); in the country this can be done at longer intervals


---


The accumulator should not be used when, nearby (i.e., up to about 10 m), a [tube-type] television set, an X-ray machine, or fluorescent tubes are in operation; furthermore not during a so-called DOR atmosphere or during a thunderstorm. Likewise one should avoid the accumulator for two to three days after an X-ray irradiation.

Whole-body irradiation
The effect of the accumulator rests on the law of the orgonomic potential: in contrast to the otherwise known physical forms of energy, which all follow the mechanical potential law (Second Law of Thermodynamics), orgone energy always flows from the weaker to the stronger system, and indeed in a genuinely self-regulatory manner, until saturation is reached. There is no rigid rule for this saturation; in each system it is reached after a different length of time. In the human user, who represents the stronger system relative to the accumulator, and to whom the concentrated energy flows, the measure of saturation presents itself through the subjective feeling that "nothing more is happening." One should then definitely end the session, since over-irradiation is unpleasant and harmful. As a very rough guideline, an accumulator session lasts about 20 to 60 minutes.

On the basis of the explained mode of operation of the accumulator, the effect is not diminished when several persons use the accumulator in quick succession: the orgone is not, as one might perhaps suspect, "used up."

Although the greatest atmospheric orgone concentration exists between 12 and 2 o'clock, the effect of the accumulator is only insignificantly more intense during this time, since the fluctuations over the day hardly matter quantitatively in comparison with the concentration in the accumulator. The same holds for humid days, on which the sessions may possibly last somewhat longer.

The use of the accumulator is no substitute for orgone therapy, but is to be regarded more as a preventive measure. The effect on any particular illness is hardly direct. A cancerous tumor, for example, is not immediately influenced; a cold will not clear up after a few sessions; a migraine or a rheumatic pain may persist over a certain time. For orgone energy acts first of all on the total organism, as it were on the ground on which the individual symptoms develop. Only when the whole organism,

www.orgonomie.net
                                         187

in particular the blood system, is charged up do the symptoms begin to wane. It is thus not the energy applied from outside that acts "healingly," but the raised level of the inner energy. The accumulator helps nature to help itself.

Local irradiation


---


While whole-body irradiation in the large accumulator acts by strengthening the entire bio-energy system, one can, by means of a small accumulator, the so-called "shooter," also carry out local irradiations, for example with cuts or burns. The orgone energy concentrated in the interior of the accumulator is thereby directed, via a flexible iron hose wrapped with insulating material (e.g., Tesa crepe), specifically to the desired places.

For small areas it suffices to hold the end of the hose at a distance of about 3 mm from the relevant spot; for larger areas, an iron funnel likewise wrapped (on the outside only) with Tesa crepe is to be attached to the end of the hose.

After some time of irradiation one feels prickling and warmth. Wherever pain is present, it begins—occasionally after a very brief period of increased intensity—to fade. With injuries one should not irradiate for longer than 15 to 20 minutes in succession. Irradiations of other parts of the body, without these being injured, should be carried out only until one feels the sensation of warmth. Such irradiations can be undertaken daily, while one sits for whole-body irradiation in the large accumulator (on top of the small one, as a bench). Recommended places are, e.g., the eyes (only with closed lids, about 1 minute), the area between the eyes, the area of the lower tip of the heart and the base of the heart, the mouth, and the solar plexus.

Although in the normal case, and with observance of the recommendations given, the application of orgone energy irradiations is harmless (for orgone is a body's own energy), Reich recommends special medical supervision in all the following cases:

(a) with all overcharge biopathies, i.e., with too-high blood pressure,
          hysterical character, acute sexual stasis; furthermore with:
          decompensated heart ailments, systolic blood pressure over 150,
          brain tumors, arteriosclerosis, previous strokes,
          inflammations of the skin and conjunctiva;

(b) with illnesses in which the influence of orgone irradiation has not
          yet been researched;

(c) in all cases of overcharge through excessive accumulator use;

(d) in all cases of the use of accumulators with more than three (double)
          layers.

www.orgonomie.net
                                       188

These notes are taken from Reich's accumulator brochure of the year 1950. Expert medical care is scarcely obtainable today. Therefore, caution is generally advised. If it feels in any way unpleasant, one should break off the irradiation.

Neuroses cannot, according to Reich, be treated with physical orgone therapy alone; only the biopathic somatic background can be improved.
